These  are Two related projects that allow these features that i want to implemnt in my own project (sqlgent)  
Wren AI Service

ai

This diagram presents the flow of the AI service provided by Wren AI, which appears to involve the interaction of a large language model (LLM), such as OpenAI's GPT, with a knowledge base to process and answer queries. Here's an overview of the process according to the graph:
Index Processing :

The process begins with rewriting Model Defined Language (MDL) to Data Definition Language (DDL) to aid the LLM in better understanding the context of the data.
Vector Database:

A vector database specializes in efficiently storing and searching vector data. The vector data is where the semantic contexts are stored in a manner that's searchable by the AI.
RAG Pipeline:

    Retrieval: This is where the Wren AI service searches for contexts related to the user's query within a vector database.
    Prompt: Wren AI Service constructs a prompt that includes both the query and the context retrieved from the vector database. This prompt is designed to facilitate the generation of a relevant and accurate response by the LLM.
    LLM: The large language model receives the prompt and generates SQL and descriptions to create a result that corresponds to the user's initial query.

Output Processing:

    Validation: The SQL generated by the LLM is validated to ensure it is executable. Wren AI service collaborate with Wren Engine to execute SQL and validate the results.
    Correction: If there is an error detected during validation, or if the SQL is not considered "good enough", the system flags this and the LLM is prompted to regenerate the output.

Overall, Wren AI service combines the natural language processing capabilities of a large language model with the precision of database querying and validation to provide a robust AI-powered query answering service.


Wren Engine

engine

Wren Engine is the open context engine behind Wren AI. It gives AI agents and data applications a structured way to understand business data, instead of working only from raw tables, columns, and ad hoc prompts.

In practice, Wren Engine sits between your data sources and the agent or application that needs to query them. It provides a modeled, governed, and execution-aware layer so agents can reason over business concepts such as customers, revenue, relationships, and reusable calculations.
What problem Wren Engine solves

Modern data systems are full of useful information, but they are rarely organized in a way that AI agents can use reliably. Raw schemas often contain:

    many overlapping tables
    inconsistent naming
    business logic hidden in SQL or dashboards
    unclear join paths and metric definitions

engine before

Giving an agent direct database access does not solve those problems. The missing piece is context: a machine-readable description of what the data means and how it should be used.

Wren Engine is designed to provide that context.
What Wren Engine does

Wren Engine combines modeling, context packaging, and query execution foundations in one open system.
1. Models business context with MDL

Wren Engine uses Modeling Definition Language (MDL) to describe:

    models that map to tables or query results
    relationships between datasets
    calculated fields and reusable logic
    views and business-facing abstractions

This turns warehouse structure into a layer that is easier for both humans and AI agents to understand.
2. Gives agents structured context instead of raw schema alone

Wren Engine is built around the idea that agents need more than semantics alone. They need context: trusted definitions, reusable logic, and a consistent way to navigate business data.

That context helps agents:

    choose the right datasets
    follow approved join paths
    reuse shared calculations
    generate more reliable SQL

3. Connects context to execution

Wren Engine is not only a metadata layer. It also serves as the execution foundation for modeled queries. Once data is described in MDL, Wren Engine can use that model to plan and generate queries against supported data sources.

This is what makes the project useful both for interactive analytics and for AI workflows that need grounded text-to-SQL behavior.
4. Supports MCP and agent-oriented workflows

Wren Engine is designed to work well with MCP clients and AI agents. It exposes business context in a form that can be shared with agent systems, so they can operate on governed data definitions instead of rediscovering logic from scratch on every request.
How Wren Engine fits into the Wren stack

Wren AI includes multiple services with different responsibilities:

    Wren UI provides the interface for connecting data, modeling, and asking questions.
    Wren AI Service handles retrieval, prompting, SQL generation, and validation with LLMs.
    Wren Engine provides the context and modeling foundation those workflows depend on.

You can think of the relationship like this:

    Wren Engine defines the business layer.
    Wren AI Service uses that layer to retrieve context and generate better SQL.
    Wren UI lets teams interact with the system.

This is why Wren Engine is a core part of Wren AI, but is also meaningful as its own open project.
Key ideas behind the project
Open context engine

Wren Engine is described as an open context engine because its job is to make business context portable, inspectable, and usable by different agentic systems. Instead of locking meaning inside one application, it provides a reusable foundation that other tools and workflows can build on.
Business-first modeling

The project focuses on modeling data the way people ask questions about it, not only the way the warehouse stores it. That makes it easier to define entities, metrics, and relationships in a form that aligns with business usage.
Better reliability for AI

Text-to-SQL systems fail when they guess incorrectly about definitions, joins, or source-of-truth tables. Wren Engine improves reliability by making those assumptions explicit in the model.
Versionable and maintainable definitions

Wren Engine projects can be managed as files, which makes modeling logic easier to review, version, and improve over time. See the Wren Project guide for how MDL projects are organized.
Typical workflow

A common Wren Engine workflow looks like this:

    Connect a supported data source.
    Define or generate an MDL project.
    Model datasets, relationships, and calculations.
    Deploy the compiled MDL.
    Use that modeled context from applications, MCP clients, or AI services.

From there, agents can query with more grounding, and teams can maintain one shared business layer instead of repeating logic across prompts and SQL.
When to use Wren Engine

Wren Engine is especially useful when you want to:

    build an agent or MCP workflow on top of business data
    create a reusable business layer across analytics tools
    improve text-to-SQL reliability with explicit modeling
    separate business logic from raw warehouse structure
    keep modeling definitions under version control


What is Modeling Definition Language (MDL)?

Modeling Definition Language (MDL) is the way Wren Engine describes business data in a structured, machine-readable form. It defines models, relationships, calculations, and views so that both humans and AI agents can work from the same business context.

Instead of exposing only raw tables and columns, MDL gives your data a logical shape. It tells Wren Engine how datasets relate to each other, how business metrics should be defined, and how analytical logic should be reused across queries.
Why MDL matters

Raw schemas are not enough for reliable analytics or AI-driven querying. A warehouse may contain hundreds of tables, inconsistent naming, and business logic scattered across dashboards or SQL scripts. MDL helps centralize that logic into a form that is easier to understand, review, and execute.

With MDL, Wren Engine can provide AI agents with the context they need to:

    understand business entities and terminology
    follow defined relationships between datasets
    reuse approved calculations and aggregations
    generate more reliable SQL from natural language

What MDL defines

MDL is used to model the business-facing structure of your data. Depending on your use case, it can define:

    models that reference physical tables or query results
    columns and their expressions
    relationships between models
    calculated fields and reusable metrics
    views built on top of modeled datasets

This gives Wren Engine a consistent representation of how your data should behave, rather than forcing every user or agent to rediscover that logic from scratch.
How MDL helps AI agents

AI agents perform better when they can reason over structured context instead of guessing from raw schema alone. MDL helps by giving Wren Engine an explicit description of your business layer.

That improves agent behavior in several ways:

    better mapping from business questions to data models
    fewer incorrect joins and ambiguous field selections
    more consistent metric definitions across queries
    clearer grounding for text-to-SQL and RAG workflows

In this sense, MDL is one of the core building blocks that lets Wren Engine act as an open context engine for AI agents.
Benefits of MDL
1. Shared definitions

MDL creates a single, reviewable place to define business logic. Teams can align on the meaning of models, relationships, and metrics instead of duplicating that logic across prompts, dashboards, and SQL files.
2. Reusable modeling logic

Once a relationship or calculation is defined in MDL, it can be reused across workflows. This reduces repeated SQL logic and makes analytical behavior more consistent.
3. Better collaboration

Because MDL is structured and explicit, it is easier for data teams to review, maintain, and improve over time. It also makes the business context more accessible to non-authors, including AI systems.
4. More reliable execution

Wren Engine can plan and generate queries more reliably when it has modeled definitions to work from. This helps reduce errors caused by incomplete schema interpretation or one-off query logic.
5. A stronger foundation for agentic analytics

If you want AI agents to operate on business data safely and accurately, they need more than access. They need context. MDL gives Wren Engine that context in a durable, portable form.
In short

MDL is the modeling language that powers Wren Engine. It turns raw data structures into usable business context, making analytics workflows easier to govern for people and easier to reason over for AI agents.

each source code exits in these path respectfully :  /home/wael/Code/WrenAI and /home/wael/Code/wren-engine,

Why Wren Engine helps AI agents

Wren Engine gives AI agents a reliable way to understand business data before they generate SQL or answer questions. Instead of asking an LLM to infer meaning directly from raw tables and column names, Wren Engine provides structured business context through MDL, relationships, metrics, and governed access patterns.

This matters because most failures in text-to-SQL systems do not come from SQL syntax alone. They come from missing context: unclear business definitions, ambiguous joins, inconsistent metric logic, and access to the wrong data. Wren Engine reduces those failures by acting as an open context engine between AI agents and your data sources.
What Wren Engine provides to LLM workflows
1. Shared business context

MDL captures the meaning of your data in a form that both humans and AI agents can use. It defines:

    business entities
    relationships between models
    reusable calculations and aggregations
    curated dataset structure

With that context in place, an agent has a better chance of mapping a question like "top customers by revenue" to the right models, joins, and metrics.
2. More reliable planning for text-to-SQL

LLMs are good at pattern matching, but they are weaker when a query depends on domain-specific modeling rules. Wren Engine improves planning by giving the agent explicit structure instead of forcing it to reconstruct business logic from raw schema alone.

This helps reduce:

    incorrect joins
    misuse of similarly named columns
    duplicated metric definitions
    brittle query generation based on incomplete schema interpretation

3. Better context for RAG and agent memory

RAG systems work best when the retrieved context is structured, relevant, and grounded in how the business actually defines data. Wren Engine gives retrieval systems higher-quality context by exposing modeled entities, documented relationships, and reusable logic instead of only raw database metadata.

This makes it easier for an agent to retrieve the right context for:

    question answering
    SQL generation
    follow-up analysis
    multi-step agent workflows

4. Consistent answers across tools and agents

When multiple AI agents, MCP clients, or applications access the same modeled context, they can reason from the same definitions. That consistency is important for teams that want a single place to define how metrics, dimensions, and relationships should behave.

Instead of each agent inventing its own interpretation, Wren Engine helps standardize the context they operate on.
5. Governed access to data

AI systems should not have unlimited freedom to reference every object in a warehouse. Wren Engine helps narrow and structure what an agent can work with by operating against modeled data definitions rather than arbitrary warehouse exploration alone.

That improves safety and governance by:

    limiting the working surface area
    making approved objects explicit
    preserving business logic in a reviewable form
    supporting controlled query generation between agents and data sources

6. Memory and self-learning

Most text-to-SQL systems treat every question as if it were the first. Wren Engine breaks that pattern with a built-in memory layer that learns from successful queries and gets better over time.

The memory system works at two levels:

    Schema context retrieval — instead of dumping the full schema into every prompt, the memory layer indexes your MDL and retrieves only the relevant models, columns, and relationships for each question. For small schemas it returns the full text; for large schemas it uses embedding search to find the right fragments.

    Query recall — every confirmed NL-SQL pair is stored as a few-shot example. When a similar question comes in later, the agent retrieves past queries as patterns to adapt rather than generating SQL from scratch. This turns user interactions into training data — the more questions you ask, the more accurate future answers become.

This self-learning loop is what separates a static semantic layer from an adaptive one. A traditional text-to-SQL pipeline has a fixed accuracy ceiling determined by the LLM's ability to interpret schema. With memory, that ceiling rises with usage: domain-specific vocabulary, unusual join patterns, and business-specific aggregation rules all get captured as confirmed examples that guide future generation.

The practical effect is fewer wrong answers over time — without retraining a model or writing custom prompts.
Why this is important for AI agents

AI agents need more than schema access. They need context they can plan with.

Wren Engine is designed for that layer of the stack: it turns raw warehouse structure into usable business context, exposes that context through MCP-friendly workflows, and helps agents generate more accurate, explainable, and governable data interactions.

If you are building AI-native analytics, text-to-SQL experiences, or agent workflows over enterprise data, Wren Engine helps bridge the gap between raw data systems and trustworthy agent behavior.

1: import asyncio

7: from langchain_core.callbacks.usage import UsageMetadataCallbackHandler
8: from langchain_core.messages import AIMessageChunk

12: from config.db_config import DBConfig

14: 
15: logging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO, format=" %(levelname)s - %(message)s")
16: 
17: 
18: def build_agent():
19:     from agent.graph import agent
20: 
21:     return agent
22: 
23: 
24: def display_streaming_content(content: str) -> None:
25:     print(content, end="", flush=True)
26: 
27: 
28: # FIX:fix prefilled text not working some environments (Different behavior of readline in Linux/Windows and terminal emulators )
29: async def get_user_input(interrupt_info) -> dict:
30:     print("\n" + "=" * 30)
31:     print("INTERRUPTED:")
32:     try:
33:         print(json.dumps(interrupt_info, indent=2))
34:     except TypeError:
35:         print(interrupt_info)
36: 
37:     prompt = "\nHow would you like to proceed? \n[a]ccept  [e]dit  [r]eject [f]eedback \nChoice: "
38:     choice = (await asyncio.to_thread(input, prompt)).strip().lower()
39: 
40:     if choice.startswith("e"):
41:         current_query = interrupt_info[0].get("args").get("query")
42: 
43:         def input_with_prefill(prompt, text):
44:             def hook():
45:                 readline.insert_text(text)
46:                 readline.redisplay()
47: 
48:             readline.set_pre_input_hook(hook)
49:             try:
50:                 return input(prompt)
51:             finally:
52:                 readline.set_pre_input_hook(None)
53: 
54:         new_query = await asyncio.to_thread(
55:             input_with_prefill, "Edit query: ", current_query
56:         )
57: 
58:         return {"type": "edit", "edited_query": new_query}
59: 
60:     elif choice.startswith("f"):
61:         feedback = await asyncio.to_thread(input, "Feedback for the agent: \n")
62:         return {"type": "response", "feedback": feedback}
63: 
64:     elif choice.startswith("r"):
65:         return {"type": "reject"}
66: 
67:     return {"type": "accept"}
68: 
69: 
70: async def run_agent(input_state: dict, config: dict):
71:     print("--- Starting Agent ---")
72:     agent = build_agent()
73: 
74:     while True:
75:         resume_required = False
76:         async for mode, chunk in agent.astream(
77:             input_state,
78:             stream_mode=["updates", "messages"],
79:             config=config,
80:         ):
81:             if mode == "messages":
82:                 msg, _ = chunk
83:                 if isinstance(msg, AIMessageChunk) and msg.content:
84:                     display_streaming_content(msg.content)
85:             if mode == "updates":
86:                 if "__interrupt__" in chunk:
87:                     interrupt_info = chunk["__interrupt__"][0].value
88:                     user_response = await get_user_input(interrupt_info)
89:                     input_state = Command(resume=user_response)
90:                     resume_required = True
91:                     print("\n--- Resuming Agent ---")
92:                     break
93:                 else:
94:                     current_node = next(iter(chunk.keys()), None)
95:                     if current_node:
96:                         print(f"\n[Transition] -> {current_node}")
97:         if not resume_required:
98:             break
99: 
100:     if AppConfig().SHOW_NODE_HISTORY:
101:         print("\n--- Full Node History ---")
102:         for i, state in enumerate(agent.get_state_history(config)):
103:             print(f"Checkpoint {i}: next={state.next} ")
104: 
105: 
106: if __name__ == "__main__":
107:     db_config = DBConfig()
108:     selected_uri = configure_database_target(db_config)
109:     print(f"Using database target: {selected_uri}")
110: 
111:     usage_callback = UsageMetadataCallbackHandler()
112:     config = {
113:         "configurable": {
114:             "thread_id": str(uuid.uuid4()),
115:             "metadata_bypass_cache": False,
116:             "metadata_invalidate_cache": False,
117:         },
118:         "callbacks": [usage_callback],
119:     }
120:     questions = {
121:         "success": "Which genre on average has the longest tracks?",
122:         "empty_result": "give me the names of all employees born after 1990-01-01",
123:         "skipped": "What is the airspeed velocity of an unladen swallow?",
124:         "complex1": "Which 5 artists generated the most revenue, and what is their total revenue and number of tracks sold?",
125:         "complex2": "Which customers spent more than the average customer spending, and what is their total amount spent?",
126:         "northwind": "Find the top 3 employees who generated the highest total revenue from orders in 1997, including the employee’s full name, total revenue, and the number of distinct customers they served. Only include orders where the total order amount exceeds $5,000.",
127:     }
128:     question = questions["complex1"]
129: 
130:     initial_state = {
131:         "messages": [{"role": "user", "content": question}],
132:         "user_question": question,
133:         "last_query": None,
134:         "previous_queries": [],
135:         "analysis_result": None,
136:         "skip_decision": None,
137:         "db_output": None,
138:         "retry_count": 0,
139:     }
140: 
141:     asyncio.run(run_agent(initial_state, config))
142: 
143:     print("\n--- Token Usage ---")
144:     print(usage_callback.usage_metadata)

1: from langgraph.checkpoint.memory import InMemorySaver
2: from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph
3: 
4: from agent.nodes import (
5:     analyze_result,
6:     call_get_schema,
7:     check_query,
8:     explain_result,
9:     generate_query,

22: builder.add_node(list_tables)
23: builder.add_node(skip_pipeline)

25: builder.add_node(get_schema_node, "get_schema")
26: builder.add_node(generate_query)
27: builder.add_node(check_query)
28: builder.add_node(run_query_node, "run_query")
29: builder.add_node(analyze_result)

34:

58:         "call_get_schema": "call_get_schema",
59:         "generate_query": "generate_query",
60:     },
61: )
62: 
63: builder.add_edge("explain_result", END)
64: 
65: checkpointer = InMemorySaver()

1: import logging
2: 
3: from langchain.messages import AIMessage
4: from langchain_core.runnables import RunnableConfig
5: from langgraph.prebuilt import ToolNode
6: 
7: from agent.prompts import (
8:     ANALYZE_RESULT,
9:     CHECK_QUERY,
10:     EXPLAIN_RESULT,
11:     GENERATE_QUERY,
12:     HANDLE_IRRELEVANT_RESULT,
13:     REGEN_PROMPTS,
14:     SHOULD_SKIP,
15: )
16: from agent.state import AgentState, AnalysisResult, SkipDecision
17: from config.app_config import AppConfig
18: from llm.model import model
19: from tools.database import (
20:     _get_config_flag,
21:     db,
22:     get_schema_tool_with_cache,
23:     list_tables_with_cache,
24:     run_query_tool_with_interrupt,
25: )
26: from utils.logger_setup import LoggerSetup
27: 
28: logger = LoggerSetup.get_logger(__name__, logging.INFO)
29: 
30: 
31: def list_tables(_state: AgentState, config: RunnableConfig | None = None):
32:     bypass_cache = _get_config_flag(
33:         config,
34:         "metadata_bypass_cache",
35:         AppConfig().METADATA_CACHE_BYPASS_DEFAULT,
36:     )
37:     invalidate_cache = _get_config_flag(config, "metadata_invalidate_cache", False)
38:     result = list_tables_with_cache(
39:         bypass_cache=bypass_cache,
40:         invalidate_cache=invalidate_cache,
41:     )
42: 
43:     logger.debug(f"List of tables in the database: {result}")
44:     if not result:
45:         raise ValueError("Failed to retrieve table names from the database.")
46: 
47:     response = AIMessage(f"Available tables: {', '.join(result)}")
48: 
49:     return {"messages": [response]}
50: 
51: 
52: get_schema_node = ToolNode([get_schema_tool_with_cache], name="get_schema")
53: 
54: 
55: def skip_pipeline(state: AgentState):
56:     system_message = {
57:         "role": "system",
58:         "content": SHOULD_SKIP,
59:     }
60:     structured_model = model.with_structured_output(SkipDecision)
61:     result = structured_model.invoke([system_message] + state["messages"])
62: 
63:     logger.debug(f"Skip Decision: {result}")
64:     return {
65:         "skip_decision": result,
66:     }
67: 
68: 
69: def should_skip(state: AgentState):
70:     if state["skip_decision"] and state["skip_decision"].skip:
71:         return "explain_result"
72: 
73:     return "call_get_schema"
74: 
75: 
76: def call_get_schema(state: AgentState):
77:     llm_with_tools = model.bind_tools([get_schema_tool_with_cache])
78: 
79:     if (
80:         state["analysis_result"] is not None
81:         and state["analysis_result"].status == "irrelevant"
82:     ):
83:         user_message = {
84:             "role": "user",
85:             "content": HANDLE_IRRELEVANT_RESULT.format(
86:                 user_question=state["user_question"],
87:                 query=state["last_query"],
88:                 database_output=state["messages"][-1].content,
89:                 explanation=state["analysis_result"].explanation,
90:             ),
91:         }
92:         response = llm_with_tools.invoke([user_message] + state["messages"])
93:         current_retry_count = state["retry_count"] + 1
94:         return {"messages": [response], "retry_count": current_retry_count}
95: 
96:     response = llm_with_tools.invoke(state["messages"])
97:     logger.debug(f"Schema Tool Response: {response}")
98:     return {"messages": [response]}
99: 
100: 
101: def generate_query(state: AgentState):
102:     system_message = {
103:         "role": "system",
104:         "content": GENERATE_QUERY.format(
105:             dialect=db.dialect,
106:             top_k=5,
107:         ),
108:     }
109:     llm_with_tools = model.bind_tools(
110:         [run_query_tool_with_interrupt], tool_choice="any"
111:     )
112: 
113:     if state["analysis_result"] is not None and state["analysis_result"].status in [
114:         "error",
115:         "empty_result",
116:     ]:
117:         original_messages = [m for m in state["messages"] if m.type == "human"]
118:         user_message = {
119:             "role": "user",
120:             "content": REGEN_PROMPTS[state["analysis_result"].status].format(
121:                 query=state["last_query"],
122:                 explanation=state["analysis_result"].explanation,
123:             ),
124:         }
125: 
126:         response = llm_with_tools.invoke(
127:             [system_message] + original_messages + [user_message]
128:         )
129: 
130:         current_retry_count = state["retry_count"] + 1
131:         return {"messages": [response], "retry_count": current_retry_count}
132: 
133:     response = llm_with_tools.invoke([system_message] + state["messages"])
134:     logger.debug(f"Generated Query: {response}")
135: 
136:     return {"messages": [response]}
137: 
138: 
139: def check_query(state: AgentState):
140:     system_message = {
141:         "role": "system",
142:         "content": CHECK_QUERY.format(dialect=db.dialect),
143:     }
144: 
145:     tool_call = state["messages"][-1].tool_calls[0]
146:     user_message = {"role": "user", "content": tool_call["args"]["query"]}
147:     llm_with_tools = model.bind_tools(
148:         [run_query_tool_with_interrupt], tool_choice="any"
149:     )
150:     response = llm_with_tools.invoke([system_message, user_message])
151:     response.id = state["messages"][-1].id
152:     last_query = response.tool_calls[0]["args"]["query"]
153: 
154:     logger.debug(f"Check Query Response: {response}")
155:     logger.debug(f"Checked Query: {last_query}")
156: 
157:     return {
158:         "messages": [response],
159:         "last_query": last_query,
160:         "previous_queries": [last_query],
161:     }
162: 
163: 
164: def should_execute(_state: AgentState):
165:     if not AppConfig().EXECUTE_SQL_QUERIES:
166:         return "explain_result"
167:     return "run_query"
168: 
169: 
170: run_query_node = ToolNode([run_query_tool_with_interrupt], name="run_query")
171: 
172: 
173: def analyze_result(state: AgentState):
174:     db_output = state["messages"][-1].content or "Empty, 0 rows returned"
175: 
176:     system_message = {
177:         "role": "system",
178:         "content": ANALYZE_RESULT.format(
179:             user_input=state["user_question"],
180:             query_executed=state["last_query"],
181:             database_output=db_output,
182:             retry_count=state["retry_count"],
183:         ),
184:     }
185:     structured_llm = model.with_structured_output(AnalysisResult)
186: 
187:     response = structured_llm.invoke([system_message])
188: 
189:     logger.debug(f"Analysis Result: {response.model_dump_json()}")
190: 
191:     return {
192:         "db_output": db_output,
193:         "analysis_result": response,
194:     }
195: 
196: 
197: def should_retry(state: AgentState):
198:     analysis_result = state.get("analysis_result")
199: 
200:     if state["retry_count"] < AppConfig().MAX_SQL_RETRIES:
201:         if analysis_result.status == "irrelevant":
202:             return "call_get_schema"
203:         if analysis_result.status in ["error", "empty_result"]:
204:             return "generate_query"
205:         # explicit exit to prevent repeated queries
206:         if (
207:             len(state["previous_queries"]) > 1
208:             and state["last_query"] in state["previous_queries"][:-1]
209:         ):
210:             return "explain_result"
211: 
212:     return "explain_result"
213: 
214: 
215: def explain_result(state: AgentState):
216:     if not AppConfig().EXECUTE_SQL_QUERIES:
217:         explanation = f"SQL execution is disabled. Skipping query . Here is last generated query: {state['last_query']}"
218:         logger.debug(f"No Execution:{explanation}")
219:         return {"messages": [AIMessage(content=explanation)]}
220: 
221:     if state["skip_decision"] and state["skip_decision"].skip:
222:         explanation = f"The agent decided to skip executing the query because: {state['skip_decision'].reason}"
223:         logger.debug(f"Skip Explanation: {explanation}")
224:         return {"messages": [AIMessage(content=explanation)]}
225: 
226:     system_message = {
227:         "role": "system",
228:         "content": EXPLAIN_RESULT.format(
229:             user_question=state["user_question"],
230:             query=state["last_query"],
231:             status=state["analysis_result"].status,
232:             database_output=state["db_output"],
233:             explanation=state["analysis_result"].explanation,
234:         ),
235:     }
236:     response = model.invoke([system_message] + state["messages"])
237:     logger.debug(f"Explanation: {response}")
238:     return {"messages": [response]}

</content>

1: GENERATE_QUERY = """
2: You are an agent designed to interact with a SQL database.
3: Given an input question, create a syntactically correct {dialect} query to run,
4: then look at the results of the query and return the answer. Unless the user
5: specifies a specific number of examples they wish to obtain, always limit your
6: query to at most {top_k} results.
7: 
8: You can order the results by a relevant column to return the most interesting
9: examples in the database. Never query for all the columns from a specific table,
10: only ask for the relevant columns given the question.
11: 
12: DO NOT make any DML statements (INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, DROP etc.) to the database.
13: 
14: IMPORTANT: You MUST always call the sql_db_query tool with your query.
15: Never respond with plain text or a final answer, always use the tool.
16: If a previous query returned no results or an error, modify the query and try again using the tool.
17: 
18: """
19: 
20: CHECK_QUERY = """
21: You are a SQL expert with a strong attention to detail.
22: Double check the {dialect} query for common mistakes, including:
23: - Using NOT IN with NULL values
24: - Using UNION when UNION ALL should have been used
25: - Using BETWEEN for exclusive ranges
26: - Data type mismatch in predicates
27: - Properly quoting identifiers
28: - Using the correct number of arguments for functions
29: - Casting to the correct data type
30: - Using the proper columns for joins
31: 
32: If there are any of the above mistakes, rewrite the query. If there are no mistakes,
33: just reproduce the original query.
34: 
35: You will call the appropriate tool to execute the query after running this check.
36: """
37: 
38: ANALYZE_RESULT = """
39: You are a Data Analyst. Evaluate the SQL execution result against the user request.
40: 
41: USER REQUEST: {user_input}
42: SQL QUERY EXECUTED: {query_executed}
43: DATABASE RESPONSE: {database_output}
44: CURRENT RETRY ATTEMPT: {retry_count}
45: 
46: EVALUATION RULES:
47: 1. SUCCESS: Data directly answers the question.
48: 2. ERROR: Database returned a syntax or execution error.
49: 3. IRRELEVANT: Data returned does not match the semantic intent of the question.
50: 4. EMPTY_RESULT: Query executed successfully but returned 0 rows.
51: 
52: INSTRUCTIONS:
53: - If SUCCESS: Explain why the query correctly answers the question.
54: - If ERROR: Explain the technical error and add the error in the explanation.
55: - If IRRELEVANT: Explain which columns/data were wrong.
56: - If EMPTY_RESULT: 
57:   - If retry_count is 0: Suggest broader filters (e.g. remove WHERE clause, use LIKE).
58:   - If retry_count > 0:  State confidently that no data exists for this request.
59: 
60: OUTPUT FORMAT:
61: Return valid JSON matching the AnalysisResult schema.
62: """
63: 
64: EXPLAIN_RESULT = """
65: You are a helpful data assistant presenting query results to a non-technical user.
66: Based on the outcome of the SQL query execution, respond appropriately using one of the cases below.
67: 
68: User question: {user_question}
69: 
70: SQL executed:
71: {query}
72: 
73: Status: {status}
74: 
75: Result:
76: {database_output}
77: 
78: Analysis:
79: {explanation}
80: 
81: ---
82: 
83: Follow these instructions based on the status:
84: 
85: - success: Present the results clearly and concisely in natural language. Summarize key findings, highlight important values, and format tables or lists if needed.
86: 
87: - empty_result: Inform the user that the query ran successfully but returned no matching data. Suggest that the data they are looking for may not exist in the database, or that their question may need to be rephrased.
88: 
89: - irrelevant: Show the returned data to the user and explain that while the query executed, the results may not fully match their intent. Ask the user if this is close to what they were looking for or if they would like to refine the question.
90: 
91: - error: Apologize and inform the user that the query could not be executed due to an internal error after multiple attempts. Avoid technical jargon. Suggest they rephrase their question or contact support.
92: 
93: Always be concise, friendly, and avoid exposing raw SQL or technical error messages to the user.
94: """
95: 
96: 
97: REGENERATE_QUERY_ON_ERROR = """
98: The previous query produced an error.
99: 
100: SQL:
101: {query}
102: 
103: Error:
104: {explanation}
105: 
106: Fix the SQL query. Do not repeat the same mistake.
107: 
108: """
109: 
110: REGENERATE_QUERY_ON_EMPTY_RESULT = """
111: The previous query executed successfully but returned no results."
112: 
113: SQL:{query}"
114: 
115: Analysis:{explanation}"
116: 
117: Reconsider your assumptions — the filters, joins, or conditions may be too restrictive. "
118: Generate a revised query that is more likely to return data."

3: from langchain.tools import tool

24: 
25: ALLOWED = (
26:     exp.Select,
27:     exp.With,
28: )
29: 
30: # SQLAlchemy and sqlglot use differant  names for some dialects
31: SQLGLOT_COMPLIANET_DIALECTS = {
32:     "mssql": "tsql",
33:     "postgresql": "postgres",

35: }
36: 
37: 
38: def validate_sql(query: str, dialect: str = None) -> bool:
39:     try:
40:         statements = parse(query, read=dialect)
41: 
42:     except Exception as e:
43:         logger.error(f"Parser Error: {e}")
44:         return False
45: 
46:     if len(statements) != 1:
47:         return False
48: 
49:     ast = statements[0]
50: 
51:     if not isinstance(ast, ALLOWED):
52:         return False
53: 
54:     return True
55: 
56: 
57: def _build_database_components():
58:     database_uri = DBConfig().get_database_uri()
59:     database = SQLDatabase.from_uri(database_uri, sample_rows_in_table_info=1)
60:     get_schema = InfoSQLDatabaseTool(db=database)
61:     run_query = QuerySQLDatabaseTool(db=database)
62: 
63:     return database, get_schema, run_query
64: 
65: 
66: db, get_schema_tool, run_query_tool = _build_database_components()
67: 
68: 
69: def _get_config_flag(
70:     config: RunnableConfig | None, flag: str, default: bool = False
71: ) -> bool:
72:     if not config:
73:         return default
74:     configurable = config.get("configurable") if isinstance(config, dict) else None
75:     if not isinstance(configurable, dict):
76:         return default
77:     return bool(configurable.get(flag, default))
78: 
79: 
80: def list_tables_with_cache(
81:     *, bypass_cache: bool = False, invalidate_cache: bool = False
82: ) -> list[str]:
83:     if invalidate_cache:
84:         invalidate_metadata_cache(DBConfig().get_database_uri())
85: 
86:     cached = get_cached_metadata(
87:         operation="list_tables",
88:         operation_args=None,
89:         bypass_cache=bypass_cache,
90:     )
91:     if cached is not None:
92:         return list(cached)
93: 
94:     table_names = db.get_usable_table_names()
95:     set_cached_metadata(
96:         operation="list_tables",
97:         operation_args=None,
98:         value=table_names,

100:     )
101:     return table_names

111:     operation_args = {"table_names": tool_input.get("table_names", "")}

135: )
136: def run_query_tool_with_interrupt(config: RunnableConfig, **tool_input):
137:     # static check
138:     dialect = SQLGLOT_COMPLIANET_DIALECTS.get(db.dialect, db.dialect)
139:     if not validate_sql(tool_input["query"], dialect=dialect):
140:         raise ValueError(
141:             "Failed the static check. Only SELECT and WITH statements are allowed. No multiple statements allowed."
142:         )
143:     # human interruption
144:     if not AppConfig().HUMAN_SQL_REVIEW:
145:         final_query_input = tool_input
146:     else:
147:         request = {
148:             "action": run_query_tool.name,
149:             "args": tool_input,
150:             "description": "Please review the tool call",
151:         }
152:         response = interrupt([request])
153:         if response["type"] == "accept":
154:             final_query_input = tool_input
155:         elif response["type"] == "edit":
156:             tool_input = response["edited_query"]
157:             final_query_input = {"query": tool_input}
158:         elif response["type"] == "response":
159:             return f"User cancelled the query and provided this feedback: {response['feedback']}"
160: 
161:         elif response["type"] == "reject":
162:             raise RuntimeError("User rejected the tool call")
163:         else:
164:             raise ValueError(f"Unsupported interrupt response type: {response['type']}")
165:     try:
166:         tool_response = run_query_tool.invoke(final_query_input, config)
167:         return tool_response
168: 
169:     except Exception as e:
170:         return (
171:             f"Error executing SQL: {str(e)}\n"
172:             "Please analyze this error, correct the query, and try again."
173:         )

1: import hashlib
2: import json
3: import logging
4: import sqlite3

6: import time
7: from pathlib import Path

17: class MetadataCacheStore:
18:     def __init__(self, cache_path: Path):
19:         self._cache_path = cache_path
20:         self._lock = threading.Lock()
21:         self._ensure_db()
22: 
23:     def _connect(self) -> sqlite3.Connection:
24:         return sqlite3.connect(self._cache_path, timeout=5)
25: 
26:     def _ensure_db(self) -> None:
27:         self._cache_path.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
28:         with self._connect() as conn:
29:             conn.execute(
30:                 """
31:                 CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS metadata_cache (
32:                     cache_key TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
33:                     db_fingerprint TEXT NOT NULL,
34:                     operation TEXT NOT NULL,
35:                     args_hash TEXT NOT NULL,
36:                     value_json TEXT NOT NULL,
37:                     created_at INTEGER NOT NULL,
38:                     expires_at INTEGER
39:                 )
40:                 """
41:             )
42:             conn.execute(
43:                 """
44:                 CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS idx_metadata_cache_db_operation
45:                 ON metadata_cache (db_fingerprint, operation)
46:                 """
47:             )
48:             conn.execute(
49:                 """
50:                 CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS idx_metadata_cache_expires
51:                 ON metadata_cache (expires_at)
52:                 """
53:             )
54: 
55:     def get(self, cache_key: str) -> Any | None:
56:         with self._lock:
57:             with self._connect() as conn:
58:                 row = conn.execute(
59:                     "SELECT value_json, expires_at FROM metadata_cache WHERE cache_key = ?",
60:                     (cache_key,),
61:                 ).fetchone()
62:                 if row is None:
63:                     return None
64: 
65:                 value_json, expires_at = row
66:                 now = int(time.time())
67:                 if expires_at is not None and expires_at <= now:
68:                     conn.execute(
69:                         "DELETE FROM metadata_cache WHERE cache_key = ?", (cache_key,)
70:                     )
71:                     return None
72: 
73:                 return json.loads(value_json)
74: 
75:     def set(
76:         self,
77:         *,
78:         cache_key: str,
79:         db_fingerprint: str,
80:         operation: str,
81:         args_hash: str,
82:         value: Any,
83:         ttl_seconds: int | None,
84:     ) -> None:
85:         now = int(time.time())
86:         expires_at = (
87:             now + ttl_seconds if ttl_seconds is not None and ttl_seconds > 0 else None
88:         )
89: 
90:         with self._lock:
91:             with self._connect() as conn:
92:                 conn.execute(
93:                     """
94:                     INSERT INTO metadata_cache (
95:                         cache_key,
96:                         db_fingerprint,
97:                         operation,
98:                         args_hash,
99:                         value_json,
100:                         created_at,
101:                         expires_at
102:                     ) VALUES (?, ?, ?, ?, ?, ?, ?)
103:                     ON CONFLICT(cache_key) DO UPDATE SET
104:                         value_json=excluded.value_json,
105:                         created_at=excluded.created_at,
106:                         expires_at=excluded.expires_at
107:                     """,
108:                     (
109:                         cache_key,
110:                         db_fingerprint,
111:                         operation,
112:                         args_hash,
113:                         json.dumps(value),
114:                         now,
115:                         expires_at,
116:                     ),
117:                 )
118: 
119:     def invalidate(self, db_fingerprint: str | None = None) -> int:
120:         with self._lock:
121:             with self._connect() as conn:
122:                 if db_fingerprint is None:
123:                     cursor = conn.execute("DELETE FROM metadata_cache")
124:                 else:
125:                     cursor = conn.execute(
126:                         "DELETE FROM metadata_cache WHERE db_fingerprint = ?",
127:                         (db_fingerprint,),
128:                     )
129:                 return cursor.rowcount
130: 
131: 
132: def _hash(value: str) -> str:
133:     return hashlib.sha256(value.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
134: 
135: 
136: def _normalize_args(operation_args: dict[str, Any] | None) -> str:
137:     payload = operation_args or {}
138:     return json.dumps(payload, sort_keys=True, separators=(",", ":"))
139: 
140: 
141: def get_db_fingerprint(database_uri: str | None = None) -> str:
142:     uri = database_uri or DBConfig().get_database_uri()
143:     return _hash(uri)
144: 
145: 
146: def build_cache_key(
147:     *,
148:     database_uri: str | None,
149:     operation: str,
150:     operation_args: dict[str, Any] | None,
151: ) -> tuple[str, str, str]:
152:     db_fingerprint = get_db_fingerprint(database_uri)
153:     normalized_args = _normalize_args(operation_args)
154:     args_hash = _hash(normalized_args)
155:     cache_key = _hash(f"{db_fingerprint}|{operation}|{args_hash}")
156:     return cache_key, db_fingerprint, args_hash
157: 
158: 
159: _cache_store = MetadataCacheStore(AppConfig().METADATA_CACHE_PATH)
160: 
161: 
162: def get_cached_metadata(
163:     *,
164:     operation: str,
165:     operation_args: dict[str, Any] | None = None,
166:     database_uri: str | None = None,
167:     bypass_cache: bool = False,
168: ) -> Any | None:
169:     if not AppConfig().METADATA_CACHE_ENABLED:
170:         return None
171: 
172:     if AppConfig().METADATA_CACHE_BYPASS_DEFAULT or bypass_cache:
173:         logger.debug(f"metadata cache bypassed for operation={operation}")
174:         return None
175: 
176:     cache_key, _, _ = build_cache_key(
177:         database_uri=database_uri,
178:         operation=operation,
179:         operation_args=operation_args,
180:     )
181:     cached = _cache_store.get(cache_key)
182:     if cached is None:
183:         logger.debug(f"metadata cache miss for operation={operation}")
184:     else:
185:         logger.debug(f"metadata cache hit for operation={operation}")
186:     return cached
187: 
188: 
189: def set_cached_metadata(
190:     *,
191:     operation: str,
192:     value: Any,
193:     operation_args: dict[str, Any] | None = None,
194:     database_uri: str | None = None,
195:     bypass_cache: bool = False,
196: ) -> None:
197:     if not AppConfig().METADATA_CACHE_ENABLED:
198:         return
199: 
200:     if AppConfig().METADATA_CACHE_BYPASS_DEFAULT or bypass_cache:
201:         logger.debug(
202:             f"metadata cache write skipped by bypass for operation={operation}"
203:         )
204:         return
205: 
206:     cache_key, db_fingerprint, args_hash = build_cache_key(
207:         database_uri=database_uri,
208:         operation=operation,
209:         operation_args=operation_args,
210:     )
211:     _cache_store.set(
212:         cache_key=cache_key,
213:         db_fingerprint=db_fingerprint,
214:         operation=operation,
215:         args_hash=args_hash,
216:         value=value,
217:         ttl_seconds=AppConfig().METADATA_CACHE_TTL_SECONDS,
218:     )
219: 
220: 
221: def invalidate_metadata_cache(database_uri: str | None = None) -> int:
222:     db_fingerprint = None if database_uri is None else get_db_fingerprint(database_uri)
223:     deleted = _cache_store.invalidate(db_fingerprint)
224:     target = "all databases" if db_fingerprint is None else "active database"
225:     logger.info(f"metadata cache invalidated for {target}; entries deleted={deleted}")
226:     return deleted

34: def _build_initial_state(
35:     question: str,
36: ) -> AgentState:
37:     return AgentState(
38:         messages=[HumanMessage(content=question)],
39:         user_question=question,
40:         last_query=None,
41:         previous_queries=[],
42:         analysis_result=None,
43:         skip_decision=None,
44:         db_output=None,
45:         retry_count=0,
46:     )
47: 
48: 
49: def _make_config(
50:     bypass_cache: bool = False,
51:     invalidate_cache: bool = False,
52: ) -> RunnableConfig:
53:     return RunnableConfig(
54:         configurable={
55:             "thread_id": str(uuid.uuid4()),
56:             "metadata_bypass_cache": bypass_cache,
57:             "metadata_invalidate_cache": invalidate_cache,
58:         },
59:         callbacks=[UsageMetadataCallbackHandler()],
60:     )
61: 
62: 
63: def _extract_content(msg: AIMessage) -> str:
64:     if isinstance(msg.content, str):
65:         return msg.content
66:     parts = [
67:         block if isinstance(block, str) else block.get("text", "")
68:         for block in msg.content
69:     ]
70:     return "".join(parts)
71: 
72: 
73: async def _run_agent_auto_accept(
74:     initial_state: AgentState, config: RunnableConfig
75: ) -> str:
76:     input_state: AgentState | Command = initial_state
77: 
78:     while True:
79:         resume_required = False
80:         async for _, chunk in agent.astream(
81:             input_state,
82:             stream_mode="updates",
83:             config=config,
84:         ):
85:             if "__interrupt__" in chunk:
86:                 logger.info("Auto-accepting SQL execution interrupt.")
87:                 input_state = Command(resume={"type": "accept"})
88:                 resume_required = True
89:                 break
90: 
91:         if not resume_required:
92:             break
93: 
94:     final_state = agent.get_state(config)
95:     messages = final_state.values.get("messages", [])
96:     for msg in reversed(messages):
97:         if isinstance(msg, AIMessage) and msg.content:
98:             return _extract_content(msg)
99: 
100:     return "Agent completed but produced no output."
101: 
102: 
103: @mcp.tool()
104: async def ask_database(
105:     question: str,
106:     bypass_cache: bool = False,
107:     invalidate_cache: bool = False,
108: ) -> str:
109:     """
110:     Answer a natural language question about the database.
111: 
112:     Runs the full SQL agent pipeline: table discovery, schema retrieval,
113:     query generation, validation, execution, and result explanation.
114:     Returns a natural language answer.
115: 
116:     Args:
117:         question: A natural language question about the data.
118: 
119:     Returns:
120:         A natural language answer based on the query results.
121:     """
122:     logger.info(f"ask_database called with: {question!r}")
123:     config = _make_config(
124:         bypass_cache=bypass_cache,
125:         invalidate_cache=invalidate_cache,
126:     )
127:     initial_state = _build_initial_state(question)
128:     return await _run_agent_auto_accept(initial_state, config)
129: 
130: 
131: @mcp.tool()
132: async def generate_sql(
133:     question: str,
134:     bypass_cache: bool = False,
135:     invalidate_cache: bool = False,
136: ) -> str:
137:     """
138:     Generate a validated SQL query for a natural language question without executing it.
139: 
140:     Runs the agent pipeline through query generation and validation, then stops.
141:     Does not execute the query against the database.
142: 
143:     Args:
144:         question: A natural language question about the data.
145: 
146:     Returns:
147:         A validated SQL query string.
148:     """
149:     logger.info(f"generate_sql called with: {question!r}")
150: 
151:     from config.app_config import AppConfig
152: 
153:     original_default = AppConfig.__dataclass_fields__["EXECUTE_SQL_QUERIES"].default
154:     AppConfig.__dataclass_fields__["EXECUTE_SQL_QUERIES"].default = False
155: 
156:     try:
157:         config = _make_config(
158:             bypass_cache=bypass_cache,
159:             invalidate_cache=invalidate_cache,
160:         )
161:         initial_state = _build_initial_state(question)
162: 
163:         async for _ in agent.astream(
164:             initial_state,
165:             stream_mode="updates",
166:             config=config,
167:         ):
168:             pass
169: 
170:         final_state = agent.get_state(config)
171:         last_query = final_state.values.get("last_query")
172: 
173:         if not last_query:
174:             return "Could not generate a SQL query for the given question."
175: 
176:         return last_query
177:     finally:
178:         AppConfig.__dataclass_fields__["EXECUTE_SQL_QUERIES"].default = original_default
179: 
180:

182: async def invalidate_cache(scope: str = "current") -> str:

186:     Args:

206:     )

1: # UI IDeas
2: - Chat interface with agent 
3: - select older converstation
4: - SEPRATE TAB: dashboard for graphs 
5: - SEPRATE TAB: maybe create a manual typing interface for manual sql writing and execution (QUERY EDITOR LIKE AZURE PORTAL) 
6: - token counting and usage 
7: - database output rows on the side 
8: - see most asked database questions and their results
9: - put toggles for SQL_EXECUTION , HUMAN_IN_THE_LOOP
10: # Old ideas:
11: ## Hot
12: - context engineering (look at WrenAI MDL and RAG)
13: 
14: - create CLI ver and self hosted UI ver for this SQL AI agent
15: - Support distributed databases(Views may not contain full db schema sometimes).
16: - DB dialect Prober (MySQL, PostgresSQL, etc) 
17: - Docker DBs, Local DBs, Cloud DBs support
18: - Add autocomeplete for DB names, table names, column names 
19: - add a toggle to verify sql before executing it (show the generated SQL and ask for confirmation before executing it)
20: - add a sql injection prevention mechanism (e.g. check for certain keywords or patterns in the generated SQL and block execution if they are detected)
21: - implement a mulit agent system where we have a orchestor agent that receives the user query then calls the Text2SQL to generate 
22: - keep a register of queries  to cache results 
23: - log failed requests for manual review 
24: - safe execution sandbox for the sql queries
25: - index db schemas for fast retrival with rag 
26: - MCP integration: 
27: ```
28: MCP integration means exposing the NL→SQL agent as a tool that other applications can call.
29: Scenario.
30: The agent runs inside an MCP server connected to an internal database. The server exposes a tool like ask_database(question). When a client application calls it, the server runs the agent pipeline: generate SQL, validate it, execute it on the database, analyze the result, and return structured data with an explanation.
31: 
32: For example, a data analytics application can call:
33: ask_database("monthly revenue by product category")
34: The MCP server runs the query on the internal database and returns the results. The analytics app can then use the data to generate reports or graphs.
35: 
36: This allows multiple internal tools to reuse the same NL → SQL agent through a standard MCP interface.
37: ```
38: - k8s integration: 
39: ```
40:     Ephemeral Database Isolation (High Value)
41:     NL2SQL agents pose a security risk. Generated SQL may be destructive. Running queries against a shared persistent database is unsafe for testing or multi-tenant scenarios.
42: 
43:     Implementation: Use Kubernetes Jobs to spin up ephemeral database pods per session or per complex query.
44:     Mechanism: The agent triggers a Job. The Job initializes a DB container from a snapshot, executes the SQL, returns the result, and terminates the pod.
45:     K8s Feature Utilization: Jobs, Init Containers, Persistent Volume Claims (dynamic provisioning), Resource Quotas.
46:     Justification: Prevents data corruption, isolates workloads, demonstrates dynamic infrastructure management.
47: ```
48: - add db fragemnt support for the agent to be able to work with large databases without the need to dump the whole schema in the prompt

(End of file - total 48 lines)
</content>

<content>
1: # dbcore explanation
2: 
3: This document explains `src/dbcore` by logical flow and then maps **every Python file**.
4: 
5: You asked to avoid repeating the same explanation for replica files (for example every DB adapter), so this doc does:
6: - explain canonical patterns once,
7: - then mark replica files as "same pattern as X".
8: 
9: ## 1) Logical architecture: how everything connects
10: 
11: Flow (high level):
12: 
13: 1. `connections/domain` defines what a connection is (`ConnectionConfig`, endpoint/tunnel/auth concepts).
14: 2. `connections/providers` defines DB capabilities and plug-in registration (schema + adapter + provider metadata).
15: 3. `connections/discovery` finds candidate connections (Docker and cloud) and converts them to domain configs.
16: 4. `connections/app` orchestrates runtime actions (URL parsing, session lifecycle, persistence and credentials workflow).
17: 5. `connections/store` persists named connections and credential references.
18: 6. `shared/app` wires runtime services.
19: 7. `ui` renders forms/screens/controllers on top of app + provider + discovery layers.
20: 8. `tests` validate discovery and connection behavior.
21: 
22: Dependency direction is mostly one-way:
23: 
24: `domain -> providers -> discovery/app -> ui -> tests`
25: 
26: ## 2) Pattern primer (explained once)
27: 
28: ### Provider triplet pattern
29: 
30: Most DB providers follow the same 3-file structure:
31: 
32: - `provider.py`: registers metadata + factory + docker detector config.
33: - `adapter.py`: actual driver behavior (connect/query/introspection/dialect).
34: - `schema.py`: form/config schema fields and validation shape.
35: 
36: Canonical references:
37: 
38: - provider pattern: `src/dbcore/connections/providers/postgresql/provider.py`
39: - adapter pattern: `src/dbcore/connections/providers/postgresql/adapter.py`
40: - schema pattern: `src/dbcore/connections/providers/postgresql/schema.py`
41: 
42: All provider replicas listed later are variants of this pattern.
43: 
44: ### UI picker tab pattern
45: 
46: Connection picker tabs are split into:
47: 
48: - `tabs/connections.py` (saved connections),
49: - `tabs/docker.py` (docker-discovered),
50: - `tabs/cloud.py` (cloud-discovered),
51: - controller files that load/filter data and feed the tab views.
52: 
53: ## 3) File-by-file map (every file)
54: 
55: ## 3.1 `src/dbcore/connections` core
56: 
57: - `src/dbcore/connections/__init__.py` - package marker.
58: - `src/dbcore/connections/domain/__init__.py` - domain package marker.
59: - `src/dbcore/connections/domain/config.py` - core config models and normalization/serialization helpers.
60: - `src/dbcore/connections/domain/passwords.py` - rules for when DB/SSH passwords are required.
61: 
62: ### app orchestration
63: 
64: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/__init__.py` - app package marker.
65: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/cloud_actions.py` - executes cloud actions in connection picker flows.
66: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/connection_flow.py` - prompts/populates missing credentials and sequences connection readiness.
67: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/credentials.py` - keyring/plaintext/in-memory credential backends.
68: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/executor.py` - serialized DB operation executor.
69: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/install_strategy.py` - computes install strategy for missing drivers.
70: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/installer.py` - package installation workflow.
71: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/persist_utils.py` - persistence safety helpers (avoid accidental password loss).
72: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/save_connection.py` - save/rename/update helpers for connection configs.
73: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/session.py` - connection session lifecycle (connect/switch/close/tunnel cleanup).
74: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/tunnel.py` - SSH tunnel creation and dependency checks.
75: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/url_parser.py` - parses DB URLs into `ConnectionConfig`.
76: 
77: ### app mocks/test scaffolding
78: 
79: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/mock_adapter_core.py` - mock cursor/connection core.
80: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/mock_data.py` - fake dataset generation.
81: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/mock_default_adapters.py` - default mock adapters per DB type.
82: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/mock_profiles.py` - predefined mock connection profiles.
83: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/mock_provider.py` - mock provider factory.
84: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/mock_settings.py` - parse mock settings JSON to runtime shape.
85: - `src/dbcore/connections/app/mocks.py` - central mock profile declarations.
86: 
87: ### cli
88: 
89: - `src/dbcore/connections/cli/__init__.py` - CLI package marker.
90: - `src/dbcore/connections/cli/commands.py` - CLI commands for listing/saving/testing connections.
91: - `src/dbcore/connections/cli/helpers.py` - schema-aware CLI parsing helpers.
92: - `src/dbcore/connections/cli/prompts.py` - interactive CLI prompts for passwords/fields.
93: 
94: ### discovery
95: 
96: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/__init__.py` - discovery package marker.
97: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/docker_detector.py` - Docker scan/status/credential extraction/conversion.
98: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/__init__.py` - cloud discovery package exports.
99: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/base.py` - cloud provider interfaces and shared types.
100: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/mock.py` - mock cloud discovery states.
101: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/registry.py` - cloud provider registry.
102: 
103: #### cloud/aws
104: 
105: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/aws/__init__.py` - AWS discovery package marker.
106: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/aws/cache.py` - AWS discovery cache IO.
107: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/aws/provider.py` - AWS DB discovery/provider behavior.
108: 
109: #### cloud/azure
110: 
111: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/azure/__init__.py` - Azure discovery package marker.
112: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/azure/cache.py` - Azure discovery cache IO.
113: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/azure/cli.py` - Azure CLI wrappers.
114: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/azure/discovery.py` - Azure resource discovery logic.
115: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/azure/firewall.py` - Azure firewall helpers and error detection.
116: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/azure/models.py` - Azure discovery models.
117: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/azure/provider.py` - Azure cloud provider integration.
118: 
119: #### cloud/gcp
120: 
121: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/gcp/__init__.py` - GCP discovery package marker.
122: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/gcp/cache.py` - GCP discovery cache IO.
123: - `src/dbcore/connections/discovery/cloud/gcp/provider.py` - GCP Cloud SQL discovery/provider logic.
124: 
125: ### store
126: 
127: - `src/dbcore/connections/store/__init__.py` - store package exports.
128: - `src/dbcore/connections/store/connections.py` - persistent JSON connection store + credential interaction.
129: - `src/dbcore/connections/store/memory.py` - in-memory non-persistent store.
130: 
131: ## 3.2 `src/dbcore/connections/providers` non-replica infrastructure
132: 
133: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/__init__.py` - provider package exports.
134: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/adapter_provider.py` - wraps adapter+schema into full provider object.
135: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/catalog.py` - provider discovery/registration/catalog lookup.
136: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/config_service.py` - normalize/validate config through provider.
137: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/docker.py` - provider-side docker detector model.
138: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/driver.py` - driver import checks and missing-driver behavior.
139: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/exceptions.py` - provider/driver exception types.
140: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/explorer_nodes.py` - explorer node provider abstraction for UI tree.
141: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/metadata.py` - convenience accessors for provider metadata.
142: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/model.py` - provider protocols/spec/capability data model.
143: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/registry.py` - compatibility shim over catalog APIs.
144: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/schema_catalog.py` - schema catalog compatibility layer.
145: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/schema_helpers.py` - schema field builders and common schema pieces.
146: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/tls.py` - TLS-related normalization/options helpers.
147: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/validation.py` - schema-driven config validator.
148: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/adapters/__init__.py` - adapter base package marker.
149: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/adapters/base.py` - base adapter class and shared adapter contracts.
150: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/postgresql/base.py` - PostgreSQL-family shared behavior.
151: - `src/dbcore/connections/providers/mysql/base.py` - MySQL-family shared behavior.
152: 
153: ## 3.3 `src/dbcore/connections/providers` provider replicas (all files)
154: 
155: Replica note: each set below follows the canonical provider/adapter/schema pattern.

<type>file</type>
<content>
1: Awesome request. HereÔÇÖs a practical map of `src/dbcore/` so you know what each part is for and when itÔÇÖs used.
2: 
3: **Big Picture**
4: - `connections/` = DB connection domain (models, provider adapters, discovery, persistence, CLI workflows).
5: - `ui/` = Textual screens/controllers/widgets for connection setup and runtime connection actions.
6: - `shared/` = cross-cutting runtime/service/container/core utilities reused by both UI + backend flows.
7: - `domains/` = higher-level feature domains (query history/starred/settings, explorer tree, shell scheduler).
8: - `tests/` = unit + integration tests (mostly Docker/cloud/connection behavior).
9: 
10: **How Code Flows (Typical)**
11: - UI screen (`ui/screens/connection.py`) collects inputs.
12: - Field/schema definitions from provider layer (`connections/providers/*/schema.py`).
13: - Validation/config normalize (`connections/providers/validation.py`, `config_service.py`).
14: - Save/persist via app services + store (`shared/app/services.py`, `connections/store/connections.py`).
15: - Connect/test executes via provider adapter (`connections/providers/*/adapter.py`) and session/tunnel (`connections/app/session.py`, `tunnel.py`).
16: 
17: ---
18: 
19: **`connections/domain/` (Core models)**
20: - `config.py`: primary connection dataclasses/enums (`ConnectionConfig`, endpoints, auth, etc.).  
21:   Used everywhere.
22: - `passwords.py`: password prompt/storage policy helpers.
23: 
24: **`connections/providers/` (DB-specific engine)**
25: This is the biggest area and follows a pattern per database:
26: - `*/schema.py`: what fields the UI asks for (host, port, SSL, token...).
27: - `*/adapter.py`: how to connect/query that DB driver.
28: - `*/provider.py`: registration/metadata for that DB type.
29: Examples: `postgresql/`, `mysql/`, `sqlite/`, `snowflake/`, `oracle/`, `redshift/`, etc.
30: Shared provider infrastructure:
31: - `model.py`, `catalog.py`, `registry.py`, `metadata.py`: provider lookup + capabilities.
32: - `driver.py`: optional dependency detection + driver status.
33: - `validation.py`, `schema_helpers.py`, `config_service.py`: normalize/validate from UI to domain.
34: - `tls.py`, `docker.py`, `exceptions.py`, `explorer_nodes.py`: support helpers.
35: 
36: **`connections/app/` (Application services/use-cases)**
37: - `credentials.py`: keyring/plaintext credential persistence rules.
38: - `session.py`: owns live DB connection lifecycle + cleanup.
39: - `tunnel.py`: SSH tunnel creation/management.
40: - `installer.py`, `install_strategy.py`: suggest/install missing Python drivers.
41: - `connection_flow.py`, `save_connection.py`, `persist_utils.py`: save/update flow orchestration.
42: - `url_parser.py`: parse connection URLs into config.
43: - `cloud_actions.py`: cloud picker action dispatch.
44: - `executor.py`: serialized DB operation execution.
45: - `mock_*` files: demo/testing profiles + fake adapters/providers/settings.
46: 
47: **`connections/discovery/` (Auto-detection)**
48: - `docker_detector.py`: scans containers and maps to connection candidates.
49: - `cloud/*`: AWS/Azure/GCP discovery abstraction + provider impl + caches + models + firewall helpers.
50: 
51: **`connections/store/` (Persistence)**
52: - `connections.py`: read/write saved connections JSON.
53: - `memory.py`: in-memory store for tests/mock mode.
54: 
55: **`connections/cli/`**
56: - `commands.py`, `helpers.py`, `prompts.py`: CLI flows for connection management.
57: 
58: ---
59: 
60: **`ui/` (Textual interaction layer)**
61: Core logic:
62: - `connection_form.py`, `fields.py`, `field_widgets.py`: render and bind provider schema fields.
63: - `validation.py`, `validation_ui_binder.py`: show field errors/valid states.
64: - `connection_test_controller.py`: run ÔÇ£test connectionÔÇØ workflow.
65: - `driver_status_controller.py`, `driver_status.py`: dependency status text + checks.
66: - `connection_error_handlers.py`, `connection_focus.py`: UX error/focus behavior.
67: - `mixins/connection.py`: central connection behavior mixin used by app screens.
68: 
69: Screens:
70: - `screens/connection.py`: main add/edit connection screen.
71: - `screens/connection_picker/*`: picker tabs (saved/docker/cloud), controllers, filtering, shortcuts, state.
72: - `screens/package_setup.py`, `install_progress.py`: missing-driver install UX.
73: - `screens/password_input.py`, `folder_input.py`, `azure_firewall.py`.
74: - `screens/confirm.py`, `error.py`, `loading.py`, `message.py`, `file_picker.py`: modal utilities.
75: - `connection_styles.py`: Textual CSS-like styles.
76: Support:
77: - `widgets.py`, `spinner.py`, `protocols.py`, `restart_cache.py`.
78: 
79: Note: a few of these (`widgets.py`, `spinner.py`, some domain tree/scheduler pieces) were introduced as compatibility scaffolding during migration; theyÔÇÖre valid modules but some are minimal.
80: 
81: ---
82: 
83: **`shared/` (Cross-cutting reusable layer)**
84: `shared/app/`
85: - `runtime.py`: env/runtime flags, startup knobs.
86: - `services.py`: DI/service container; wires stores/providers/credentials/discovery.
87: - `startup_profiler.py`: startup/import timing logging.
88: - `__init__.py`: exports common app service APIs.
89: 
90: `shared/core/`
91: - `store.py`: JSON file store base + config dir.
92: - `system_probe.py`, `system_probe_fake.py`: env/install probe + fake for tests.
93: - `processes.py`: sync/async subprocess protocols/runners.
94: - `protocols.py`: structural protocols for service dependencies.
95: - `debug_events.py`: lightweight debug event bus.
96: - `utils.py`: fuzzy match/highlight + misc helpers.
97: 
98: ---
99: 
100: **`domains/` (Feature domains above core connections)**
101: - `domains/query/store/{history,starred,memory}.py`: query history + starred query persistence.
102: - `domains/shell/store/settings.py`: app settings persistence.
103: - `domains/shell/app/idle_scheduler.py`: idle callback scheduler primitive for UI work.
104: - `domains/explorer/ui/tree/{builder,db_switching}.py`: tree refresh/label update hooks (currently lightweight/no-op placeholders).
105: 
106: ---
107: 
108: **`tests/`**
109: - `tests/unit/*`: focused logic tests (Docker filtering/credentials parsing).
110: - `tests/integration/docker_detect/*`: real/container-based detection behavior.
111: - top-level `test_database_docker.py`, `test_docker_detector.py`: shared integration coverage.
112: 
113: ---
114: 
115: If you want next, I can generate a compact ÔÇ£ownership mapÔÇØ by file (who should touch what) so you know exactly where to edit for each feature (e.g., add new DB provider, change field validation, alter picker behavior, change credential storage).
116: 
117: ---
118: 

(End of file - total 118 lines)
</content>

1: # AI Service of Wren AI
2: 
3: ## Concepts
4: 
5: Please read the [documentation](https://docs.getwren.ai/oss/concept/wren_ai_service) here to understand the concepts of Wren AI Service.
6: 
7: ## Setup for Local Development
8: 
9: ### Prerequisites
10: 
11: 1. **Python**: Install Python 3.12.\*
12: 
13:    - Recommended: Use [`pyenv`](https://github.com/pyenv/pyenv?tab=readme-ov-file#installation) to manage Python versions
14: 
15: 2. **Poetry**: Install Poetry 1.8.3
16: 
17:    ```bash
18:    curl -sSL https://install.python-poetry.org | python3 - --version 1.8.3
19:    ```
20: 
21: 3. **Just**: Install [Just](https://github.com/casey/just?tab=readme-ov-file#packages) command runner (version 1.36 or higher)
22: 
23: ### Step-by-Step Setup
24: 
25: 1. **Install Dependencies**:
26: 
27:    ```bash
28:    poetry install
29:    ```
30: 
31: 2. **Generate Configuration Files**:
32: 
33:    ```bash
34:    just init
35:    ```
36: 
37:    This creates both `.env.dev` and `config.yaml`. Use `just init --non-dev` to generate only `config.yaml`.
38: 
39:     > For Windows, add the line `set shell:= ["bash", "-cu"]` at the start of the Justfile.
40: 
41: 4. **Configure Environment**:
42: 
43:    - Edit `.env.dev` to set environment variables
44:    - Modify `config.yaml` to configure components, pipelines, and other settings
45:    - Refer to [AI Service Configuration](./docs/configuration.md) for detailed setup instructions
46: 
47: 5. **Set Up Development Environment** (optional):
48: 
49:    - Install pre-commit hooks:
50: 
51:      ```bash
52:      poetry run pre-commit install
53:      ```
54: 
55:    - Run initial pre-commit checks:
56: 
57:      ```bash
58:      poetry run pre-commit run --all-files
59:      ```
60: 
61: 6. **Run Tests** (optional):
62: 
63:    ```bash
64:    just test
65:    ```
66: 
67: ### Starting the Service
68: 
69: 1. **Start Required Containers**:
70: 
71:    ```bash
72:    just up
73:    ```
74: 
75: 2. **Launch the AI Service**:
76: 
77:    ```bash
78:    just start
79:    ```
80: 
81: 3. **Access the Service**:
82: 
83:    - API Documentation: `http://WREN_AI_SERVICE_HOST:WREN_AI_SERVICE_PORT` (default: <http://localhost:5556>)
84:    - User Interface: `http://WREN_UI_HOST:WREN_UI_PORT` (default: <http://localhost:3000>)
85: 
86: 4. **Stop the Service**:
87:    When finished, stop the containers:
88: 
89:    ```bash
90:    just down
91:    ```
92: 
93: This setup ensures a consistent development environment and helps maintain code quality through pre-commit hooks and tests. Follow these steps to get started with local development of the Wren AI Service.
94: 
95: ## Others
96: 
97: ### Pipeline Evaluation
98: 
99: For a comprehensive understanding of how to evaluate the pipelines, please refer to the [evaluation framework](./eval/README.md). This document provides detailed guidelines on the evaluation process, including how to set up and run evaluations, interpret results, and utilize the evaluation metrics effectively. It is a valuable resource for ensuring that the evaluation is conducted accurately and that the results are meaningful.
100: 
101: ### Estimate the Speed of the Pipeline(may be outdated)
102: 
103: - to run the load test
104:   - setup `DATASET_NAME` in `.env.dev`
105:   - adjust test config if needed
106:     - adjust user count in `tests/locust/config_users.json`
107:   - in wren-ai-service folder, run `just up` to start the docker containers
108:   - in wren-ai-service folder, run `just start` to start the ai service
109:   - run `just load-test`
110:   - check reports in /outputs/locust folder, there are 3 files with filename **locust*report*{test_timestamp}**:
111:     - .json: test report in json format, including info like llm provider, version
112:     - .html: test report in html format, showing tables and charts
113:     - .log: test log
114: 
115: ## Contributing
116: 
117: Thank you for investing your time in contributing to our project! Please [read this for more information](CONTRIBUTING.md)!

(End of file - total 117 lines)
</content>
