<content>
1: # What is Modeling Definition Language (MDL)?
2: 
3: Modeling Definition Language (MDL) is the way Wren Engine describes business data in a structured, machine-readable form. It defines models, relationships, calculations, and views so that both humans and AI agents can work from the same business context.
4: 
5: Instead of exposing only raw tables and columns, MDL gives your data a logical shape. It tells Wren Engine how datasets relate to each other, how business metrics should be defined, and how analytical logic should be reused across queries.
6: 
7: ## Why MDL matters
8: 
9: Raw schemas are not enough for reliable analytics or AI-driven querying. A warehouse may contain hundreds of tables, inconsistent naming, and business logic scattered across dashboards or SQL scripts. MDL helps centralize that logic into a form that is easier to understand, review, and execute.
10: 
11: With MDL, Wren Engine can provide AI agents with the context they need to:
12: 
13: - understand business entities and terminology
14: - follow defined relationships between datasets
15: - reuse approved calculations and aggregations
16: - generate more reliable SQL from natural language
17: 
18: ## What MDL defines
19: 
20: MDL is used to model the business-facing structure of your data. Depending on your use case, it can define:
21: 
22: - models that reference physical tables or query results
23: - columns and their expressions
24: - relationships between models
25: - calculated fields and reusable metrics
26: - views built on top of modeled datasets
27: 
28: This gives Wren Engine a consistent representation of how your data should behave, rather than forcing every user or agent to rediscover that logic from scratch.
29: 
30: ## How MDL helps AI agents
31: 
32: AI agents perform better when they can reason over structured context instead of guessing from raw schema alone. MDL helps by giving Wren Engine an explicit description of your business layer.
33: 
34: That improves agent behavior in several ways:
35: 
36: - better mapping from business questions to data models
37: - fewer incorrect joins and ambiguous field selections
38: - more consistent metric definitions across queries
39: - clearer grounding for text-to-SQL and RAG workflows
40: 
41: In this sense, MDL is one of the core building blocks that lets Wren Engine act as an open context engine for AI agents.
42: 
43: ## Benefits of MDL
44: 
45: ### 1. Shared definitions
46: 
47: MDL creates a single, reviewable place to define business logic. Teams can align on the meaning of models, relationships, and metrics instead of duplicating that logic across prompts, dashboards, and SQL files.
48: 
49: ### 2. Reusable modeling logic
50: 
51: Once a relationship or calculation is defined in MDL, it can be reused across workflows. This reduces repeated SQL logic and makes analytical behavior more consistent.
52: 
53: ### 3. Better collaboration
54: 
55: Because MDL is structured and explicit, it is easier for data teams to review, maintain, and improve over time. It also makes the business context more accessible to non-authors, including AI systems.
56: 
57: ### 4. More reliable execution
58: 
59: Wren Engine can plan and generate queries more reliably when it has modeled definitions to work from. This helps reduce errors caused by incomplete schema interpretation or one-off query logic.
60: 
61: ### 5. A stronger foundation for agentic analytics
62: 
63: If you want AI agents to operate on business data safely and accurately, they need more than access. They need context. MDL gives Wren Engine that context in a durable, portable form.
64: 
65: ## In short
66: 
67: MDL is the modeling language that powers Wren Engine. It turns raw data structures into usable business context, making analytics workflows easier to govern for people and easier to reason over for AI agents.

(End of file - total 67 lines)
</content>

<path>/home/wael/Code/wren-engine/docs/concept/benefits_llm.md</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: # Why Wren Engine helps AI agents
2: 
3: Wren Engine gives AI agents a reliable way to understand business data before they generate SQL or answer questions. Instead of asking an LLM to infer meaning directly from raw tables and column names, Wren Engine provides structured business context through MDL, relationships, metrics, and governed access patterns.
4: 
5: This matters because most failures in text-to-SQL systems do not come from SQL syntax alone. They come from missing context: unclear business definitions, ambiguous joins, inconsistent metric logic, and access to the wrong data. Wren Engine reduces those failures by acting as an open context engine between AI agents and your data sources.
6: 
7: ## What Wren Engine provides to LLM workflows
8: 
9: ### 1. Shared business context
10: 
11: MDL captures the meaning of your data in a form that both humans and AI agents can use. It defines:
12: 
13: - business entities
14: - relationships between models
15: - reusable calculations and aggregations
16: - curated dataset structure
17: 
18: With that context in place, an agent has a better chance of mapping a question like "top customers by revenue" to the right models, joins, and metrics.
19: 
20: ### 2. More reliable planning for text-to-SQL
21: 
22: LLMs are good at pattern matching, but they are weaker when a query depends on domain-specific modeling rules. Wren Engine improves planning by giving the agent explicit structure instead of forcing it to reconstruct business logic from raw schema alone.
23: 
24: This helps reduce:
25: 
26: - incorrect joins
27: - misuse of similarly named columns
28: - duplicated metric definitions
29: - brittle query generation based on incomplete schema interpretation
30: 
31: ### 3. Better context for RAG and agent memory
32: 
33: RAG systems work best when the retrieved context is structured, relevant, and grounded in how the business actually defines data. Wren Engine gives retrieval systems higher-quality context by exposing modeled entities, documented relationships, and reusable logic instead of only raw database metadata.
34: 
35: This makes it easier for an agent to retrieve the right context for:
36: 
37: - question answering
38: - SQL generation
39: - follow-up analysis
40: - multi-step agent workflows
41: 
42: ### 4. Consistent answers across tools and agents
43: 
44: When multiple AI agents, MCP clients, or applications access the same modeled context, they can reason from the same definitions. That consistency is important for teams that want a single place to define how metrics, dimensions, and relationships should behave.
45: 
46: Instead of each agent inventing its own interpretation, Wren Engine helps standardize the context they operate on.
47: 
48: ### 5. Governed access to data
49: 
50: AI systems should not have unlimited freedom to reference every object in a warehouse. Wren Engine helps narrow and structure what an agent can work with by operating against modeled data definitions rather than arbitrary warehouse exploration alone.
51: 
52: That improves safety and governance by:
53: 
54: - limiting the working surface area
55: - making approved objects explicit
56: - preserving business logic in a reviewable form
57: - supporting controlled query generation between agents and data sources
58: 
59: ### 6. Memory and self-learning
60: 
61: Most text-to-SQL systems treat every question as if it were the first. Wren Engine breaks that pattern with a built-in [memory layer](../guide/memory.md) that learns from successful queries and gets better over time.
62: 
63: The memory system works at two levels:
64: 
65: - **Schema context retrieval** — instead of dumping the full schema into every prompt, the memory layer indexes your MDL and retrieves only the relevant models, columns, and relationships for each question. For small schemas it returns the full text; for large schemas it uses embedding search to find the right fragments.
66: 
67: - **Query recall** — every confirmed NL-SQL pair is stored as a few-shot example. When a similar question comes in later, the agent retrieves past queries as patterns to adapt rather than generating SQL from scratch. This turns user interactions into training data — the more questions you ask, the more accurate future answers become.
68: 
69: This self-learning loop is what separates a static semantic layer from an adaptive one. A traditional text-to-SQL pipeline has a fixed accuracy ceiling determined by the LLM's ability to interpret schema. With memory, that ceiling rises with usage: domain-specific vocabulary, unusual join patterns, and business-specific aggregation rules all get captured as confirmed examples that guide future generation.
70: 
71: The practical effect is fewer wrong answers over time — without retraining a model or writing custom prompts.
72: 
73: ## Why this is important for AI agents
74: 
75: AI agents need more than schema access. They need context they can plan with.
76: 
77: Wren Engine is designed for that layer of the stack: it turns raw warehouse structure into usable business context, exposes that context through MCP-friendly workflows, and helps agents generate more accurate, explainable, and governable data interactions.
78: 
79: If you are building AI-native analytics, text-to-SQL experiences, or agent workflows over enterprise data, Wren Engine helps bridge the gap between raw data systems and trustworthy agent behavior.

(End of file - total 79 lines)
</content>

<path>/home/wael/Code/wren-engine/docs/guide/memory.md</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: # Memory
2: 
3: Wren Engine includes a **memory layer** — a LanceDB-backed semantic index that gives AI agents the context they need to write accurate SQL. Instead of sending the entire schema to an LLM on every question, the memory layer provides targeted context: relevant tables, columns, and past query examples.
4: 
5: ## Why memory matters
6: 
7: Without memory, an AI agent must either:
8: 
9: - Receive the **full schema** in every prompt — works for small databases but quickly exceeds context limits
10: - Guess which tables are relevant — leads to hallucinated column names and wrong joins
11: 
12: The memory layer solves both problems by indexing the MDL schema and storing confirmed NL-SQL pairs. At query time it retrieves only what the agent needs.
13: 
14: ## What gets indexed
15: 
16: The memory layer manages two collections:
17: 
18: | Collection | Contents | Source | Rebuildable? |
19: |------------|----------|--------|-------------|
20: | **schema_items** | Models, columns, relationships, views, instructions | MDL manifest + `instructions.md` | Yes — `wren memory index` |
21: | **query_history** | Natural-language → SQL pairs | Stored after successful queries | No — built up over time |
22: 
23: Both collections live in `<project>/.wren/memory/` (or `~/.wren/memory/` outside a project).
24: 
25: ## Installation
26: 
27: The memory system requires the `memory` extra (included in `main`):
28: 
29: ```bash
30: pip install "wren-engine[main]"    # recommended: memory + interactive + ui
31: pip install "wren-engine[memory]"  # memory only
32: ```
33: 
34: ## Indexing the schema
35: 
36: After creating or updating your MDL project, index the schema:
37: 
38: ```bash
39: wren memory index
40: ```
41: 
42: This parses the compiled `target/mdl.json`, generates local embeddings for every schema item, and stores them in LanceDB. Re-index whenever you change models, columns, relationships, or instructions:
43: 
44: ```bash
45: wren context build
46: wren memory index
47: ```
48: 
49: Check the index status:
50: 
51: ```bash
52: wren memory status
53: # Path: /Users/you/my-project/.wren/memory
54: #   schema_items: 47 rows
55: #   query_history: 12 rows
56: ```
57: 
58: ## Fetching schema context
59: 
60: `wren memory fetch` is the primary way agents get schema context. It automatically picks the best retrieval strategy based on schema size:
61: 
62: | Schema size | Strategy | What the agent sees |
63: |-------------|----------|---------------------|
64: | Below 30,000 chars (~8K tokens) | **Full text** | Complete schema with all model-column relationships, join paths, and primary keys |
65: | Above 30,000 chars | **Embedding search** | Top-k most relevant fragments for the query |
66: 
67: ```bash
68: wren memory fetch -q "customer order price"
69: wren memory fetch -q "revenue" --type column --model orders
70: wren memory fetch -q "日期" --threshold 50000 --output json
71: ```
72: 
73: ### Why hybrid?
74: 
75: Small schemas give better results as full text — the LLM sees the complete structure rather than isolated fragments. Large schemas don't fit in a single prompt, so embedding search retrieves only what's relevant.
76: 
77: The threshold is measured in characters (not tokens) because character counting is free. The 4:1 chars-to-tokens ratio holds for English; CJK text compresses less (~1.5:1), so CJK-heavy schemas switch to search sooner — the conservative direction.
78: 
79: Override with `--threshold`:
80: 
81: ```bash
82: wren memory fetch -q "revenue" --threshold 50000   # raise for larger context windows
83: ```
84: 
85: ### Full schema without search
86: 
87: `wren memory describe` prints the entire schema as structured plain text — no embeddings or LanceDB required:
88: 
89: ```bash
90: wren memory describe
91: ```
92: 
93: ## Storing and recalling queries
94: 
95: Every successful query can be stored as a natural-language → SQL pair. These pairs serve as **few-shot examples** for future questions — the more you store, the better the agent gets at writing SQL for your domain.
96: 
97: ### Storing a query
98: 
99: ```bash
100: wren memory store \
101:   --nl "top 5 customers by revenue last quarter" \
102:   --sql "SELECT c.first_name, SUM(o.amount) AS revenue FROM customers c JOIN orders o ON c.customer_id = o.customer_id WHERE o.order_date >= '2024-10-01' GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 2 DESC LIMIT 5" \
103:   --datasource duckdb
104: ```
105: 
106: **When to store:**
107: - Query executed successfully and the result is correct
108: - There is a clear natural-language question behind the query
109: 
110: **When NOT to store:**
111: - The query failed or returned wrong results
112: - The query is exploratory / throwaway (`SELECT * FROM orders LIMIT 5`)
113: - There is no natural-language question — just raw SQL
114: 
115: ### Recalling similar queries
116: 
117: Before writing new SQL, search for similar past queries:
118: 
119: ```bash
120: wren memory recall -q "best customers"
121: wren memory recall -q "月度營收" --datasource mysql --limit 5 --output json
122: ```
123: 
124: Results are returned ranked by semantic similarity. Use them as few-shot examples — adapt the SQL pattern to the current question.
125: 
126: ## Browsing and managing pairs
127: 
128: ### Listing pairs
129: 
130: Browse all stored NL-SQL pairs with `wren memory list`:
131: 
132: ```bash
133: wren memory list                        # default: 20 rows, table format
134: wren memory list --source seed          # filter by source tag
135: wren memory list --limit 50 --offset 20 # pagination
136: wren memory list --output json          # JSON output (includes _row_id)
137: ```
138: 
139: | Flag | Default | Description |
140: |------|---------|-------------|
141: | `--source` / `-s` | (all) | Filter by source: `seed`, `user`, `view` |
142: | `--limit` / `-n` | 20 | Max rows to show |
143: | `--offset` | 0 | Skip first N rows (pagination) |
144: | `--output` / `-o` | `table` | Output format: `json` or `table` |
145: 
146: ### Forgetting pairs
147: 
148: Remove incorrect or outdated NL-SQL pairs with `wren memory forget`. Three modes:
149: 
150: | Mode | Flags | Behavior |
151: |------|-------|----------|
152: | **Interactive** | (none) or `--source` | Checkbox UI — browse, select, confirm |
153: | **By ID** | `--id N [--id M ...]` | Delete specific rows (from `list --output json`) |
154: | **Batch** | `--source TAG --force` | Delete all pairs matching a source tag |
155: 
156: ```bash
157: # Interactive: checkbox UI (requires wren-engine[interactive])
158: wren memory forget
159: wren memory forget --source seed
160: 
161: # Non-interactive: delete by ID
162: wren memory forget --id 3 --force
163: wren memory forget --id 3 --id 7 --id 12 --force
164: 
165: # Batch: delete all seed pairs (re-index will regenerate)
166: wren memory forget --source seed --force
167: ```
168: 
169: The interactive mode requires the `interactive` extra (included in `main`):
170: 
171: ```bash
172: pip install "wren-engine[main]"          # recommended
173: pip install "wren-engine[interactive]"   # interactive only
174: ```
175: 
176: If InquirerPy is not installed, the command prints a hint and suggests using `--id` mode instead.
177: 
178: **Note on `_row_id`:** Row IDs come from `wren memory list --output json`. They are positional indices and may change after deletions — always re-list before using them.
179: 
180: ## Exporting and importing pairs
181: 
182: ### Dump: export to YAML
183: 
184: Export NL-SQL pairs to a human-readable YAML file:
185: 
186: ```bash
187: wren memory dump                        # write to project queries.yml (or stdout)
188: wren memory dump --source user          # only user-confirmed pairs
189: wren memory dump -o queries.yml         # explicit output path
190: wren memory dump -o -                   # force stdout (for piping)
191: ```
192: 
193: Output format:
194: 
195: ```yaml
196: version: 1
197: exported_at: "2026-04-08T10:30:00+00:00"
198: pairs:
199:   - nl: "monthly revenue by product category"
200:     sql: |
201:       SELECT category, SUM(revenue)
202:       FROM orders
203:       GROUP BY category
204:     source: user
205:     datasource: postgres-prod
206:     created_at: "2026-04-01T08:15:00+00:00"
207: ```
208: 
209: When run inside a project directory without `-o`, dump defaults to writing `<project>/queries.yml`.
210: 
211: ### Load: import from YAML
212: 
213: Import NL-SQL pairs from a YAML file:
214: 
215: ```bash
216: wren memory load queries.yml                # skip duplicates (idempotent)
217: wren memory load queries.yml --upsert       # update sql for existing nl_query
218: wren memory load queries.yml --overwrite    # clear same-source pairs first
219: wren memory load queries.yml --dry-run      # validate only, don't write
220: ```
221: 
222: | Mode | Flag | On duplicate | Use case |
223: |------|------|-------------|----------|
224: | **Skip** | (default) | Same `(nl, sql)` → skip | Safe idempotent load |
225: | **Upsert** | `--upsert` | Same `nl_query` → replace sql | Iterating on SQL quality |
226: | **Overwrite** | `--overwrite` | Clear same-source pairs first | Full sync from file |
227: 
228: `--upsert` and `--overwrite` are mutually exclusive.
229: 
230: Embeddings are recalculated on import — the YAML file only stores text, not vectors.
231: 
232: ## Project integration: `queries.yml`
233: 
234: NL-SQL pairs can be managed as part of your project, alongside models, views, and instructions:
235: 
236: ```text
237: project_root/
238: ├── wren_project.yml
239: ├── models/
240: ├── views/
241: ├── relationships.yml
242: ├── instructions.md
243: ├── queries.yml              ← curated NL-SQL pairs
244: └── target/
245:     └── mdl.json
246: ```
247: 
248: ### Scaffolding
249: 
250: `wren context init` creates an empty `queries.yml`:
251: 
252: ```yaml
253: # Curated NL-SQL pairs for this project.
254: # These are auto-loaded into memory on `wren memory index`.
255: # Use `wren memory dump` to export pairs from memory to this file.
256: # Format: same as `wren memory dump` output.
257: version: 1
258: pairs: []
259: ```
260: 
261: ### Auto-loading on index
262: 
263: `wren memory index` automatically loads `queries.yml` from the project root after indexing the schema and generating seeds. Duplicate pairs are skipped (idempotent).
264: 
265: ```bash
266: wren memory index               # indexes schema + seeds + loads queries.yml
267: wren memory index --no-queries   # skip auto-loading queries.yml
268: ```
269: 
270: ### Typical workflow
271: 
272: ```bash
273: # 1. Agent accumulates pairs during usage
274: wren memory store --nl "..." --sql "..."
275: 
276: # 2. Export user-confirmed pairs to project
277: wren memory dump --source user
278: 
279: # 3. Review, edit SQL, commit
280: git add queries.yml && git commit -m "curate query pairs"
281: 
282: # 4. New environment: index loads everything
283: wren memory index
284: ```
285: 
286: ## Agent workflow
287: 
288: The memory layer fits into the agent's query workflow like this:
289: 
290: ```
291: User asks a question
292:   │
293:   ├── 1. wren memory recall -q "..."     → find similar past queries (few-shot examples)
294:   ├── 2. wren memory fetch -q "..."      → get relevant schema context
295:   ├── 3. Write SQL using examples + context
296:   ├── 4. wren --sql "..."                → execute
297:   │
298:   └── 5. wren memory store --nl "..." --sql "..."   → save for future recall
299: ```
300: 
301: Each stored query improves future recall accuracy — the system learns from usage.
302: 
303: ### Memory hygiene (for agents)
304: 
305: Agents should use non-interactive mode (`--id` + `--force`) for memory management:
306: 
307: ```bash
308: # Review stored pairs
309: wren memory list --output json
310: 
311: # After confirming a query is WRONG: forget then store corrected version
312: wren memory forget --id <id> --force
313: wren memory store --nl "..." --sql "..."
314: 
315: # Batch cleanup: remove all seed pairs (re-index will regenerate)
316: wren memory forget --source seed --force
317: 
318: # Backup before destructive ops
319: wren memory dump -o /tmp/backup.yml
320: ```
321: 
322: ## Housekeeping
323: 
324: ```bash
325: wren memory status              # show index stats
326: wren memory reset               # drop all tables (prompts for confirmation)
327: wren memory reset --force       # drop without confirmation
328: ```
329: 
330: ## Command reference
331: 
332: | Command | Purpose |
333: |---------|---------|
334: | `memory index` | Index MDL schema + seeds + auto-load `queries.yml` |
335: | `memory fetch` | Get schema context (full text or embedding search) |
336: | `memory describe` | Print full schema as plain text (no LanceDB needed) |
337: | `memory store` | Store a single NL-SQL pair |
338: | `memory recall` | Search past pairs by semantic similarity |
339: | `memory list` | Browse all pairs with filtering and pagination |
340: | `memory forget` | Delete pairs (interactive, by ID, or by source) |
341: | `memory dump` | Export pairs to YAML |
342: | `memory load` | Import pairs from YAML |
343: | `memory status` | Show index statistics |
344: | `memory reset` | Drop all memory tables |
345: 
346: ## Storage and version control
347: 
348: Memory files are binary (LanceDB format) and stored in `<project>/.wren/memory/`. By default this directory is gitignored.
349: 
350: - **schema_items** — fully rebuildable from `wren memory index`, safe to delete
351: - **query_history** — accumulated NL-SQL pairs, exportable via `wren memory dump`
352: 
353: Use `queries.yml` to version-control curated pairs instead of committing binary LanceDB files. The dump/load workflow avoids merge conflicts and enables code review of NL-SQL pairs.

(End of file - total 353 lines)
</content>
```

21: ## Start of Pipeline
22: @observe(capture_input=False, capture_output=False)
23: @extract_fields(dict(mdl=dict[str, Any]))
24: def validate_mdl(mdl_str: str, validator: MDLValidator) -> dict[str, Any]:
25:     res = validator.run(mdl=mdl_str)
26:     return dict(mdl=res["mdl"])
27: 
28:

30: def chunk(
31:     mdl: dict[str, Any],
32:     project_id: Optional[str] = None,
33: ) -> dict[str, Any]:
34:     addition = {"project_id": project_id} if project_id else {}
35:     data_source = mdl.get("dataSource", "local_file").lower()
36: 
37:     if data_source == "duckdb":

39:         data_source = "local_file"
40: 
41:     document = Document(
42:         id=str(uuid.uuid4()),
43:         meta={"data_source": data_source, **addition},
44:     )
45:     return {"documents": [document]}
46: 
47:

51:     cleaner: DocumentCleaner,

53: ) -> dict[str, Any]:
54:     await cleaner.run(project_id=project_id)
55:     return chunk
56: 
57: 
58: @observe(capture_input=False)
59: async def write(clean: dict[str, Any], writer: DocumentWriter) -> None:
60:     return await writer.run(documents=clean["documents"])
61: 
62: 
63: ## End of Pipeline
64:

70:         **kwargs,
71:     ) -> None:

91:     ) -> dict[str, Any]:

93:             f"Project ID: {project_id}, Project Meta Indexing pipeline is running..."
94:         )

1: import logging
2: import sys
3: import uuid
4: from typing import Any, Dict, List, Optional
5: 
6: from hamilton import base
7: from hamilton.async_driver import AsyncDriver
8: from hamilton.function_modifiers import extract_fields
9: from haystack import Document, component
10: from haystack.components.writers import DocumentWriter
11: from haystack.document_stores.types import DuplicatePolicy
12: from langfuse.decorators import observe
13: from tqdm import tqdm
14: 
15: from src.core.pipeline import BasicPipeline
16: from src.core.provider import DocumentStoreProvider, EmbedderProvider
17: from src.pipelines.indexing import AsyncDocumentWriter, DocumentCleaner, MDLValidator
18: 
19: logger = logging.getLogger("wren-ai-service")
20: 
21: 
22: @component
23: class ViewChunker:
24:     """
25:     A component that processes views from an MDL (Model Definition Language) file and converts them into Document objects.
26: 
27:     The component takes views in the following MDL format:
28:     {
29:         "views": [
30:             {
31:                 "statement": "SELECT * FROM employees",
32:                 "properties": {
33:                     "historical_queries": [], # List of previous related queries
34:                     "question": "What is the average salary of employees?", # Current query
35:                     "summary": "The average salary of employees is $50,000.", # Summary for the query
36:                     "viewId": "1234567890" # Unique identifier
37:                 }
38:             }
39:         ]
40:     }
41: 
42:     And converts each view into a Document with:
43:     - content: Concatenated string of historical queries and current question
44:     - meta: Dictionary containing:
45:         {
46:             "summary": "Generated description/answer",
47:             "statement": "SQL statement",
48:             "viewId": "Unique view identifier",
49:             "project_id": "Optional project identifier"
50:         }
51: 
52:     The Documents are then stored in the document store for later retrieval.
53:     """
54: 
55:     @component.output_types(documents=List[Document])
56:     def run(self, mdl: Dict[str, Any], project_id: Optional[str] = None) -> None:
57:         def _get_content(view: Dict[str, Any]) -> str:
58:             properties = view.get("properties", {})
59:             historical_queries = properties.get("historical_queries", [])
60:             question = properties.get("question", "")
61: 
62:             return " ".join(historical_queries + [question])
63: 
64:         def _get_meta(view: Dict[str, Any]) -> Dict[str, Any]:
65:             properties = view.get("properties", {})
66:             return {
67:                 "summary": properties.get("summary", ""),
68:                 "statement": view.get("statement", ""),
69:                 "viewId": properties.get("viewId", ""),
70:             }
71: 
72:         def _additional_meta() -> Dict[str, Any]:
73:             return {"project_id": project_id} if project_id else {}
74: 
75:         chunks = [
76:             {
77:                 "id": str(uuid.uuid4()),
78:                 "content": _get_content(view),
79:                 "meta": {**_get_meta(view), **_additional_meta()},
80:             }
81:             for view in mdl["views"]
82:         ]
83: 
84:         return {
85:             "documents": [
86:                 Document(**chunk)
87:                 for chunk in tqdm(
88:                     chunks,
89:                     desc=f"Project ID: {project_id}, Chunking views into documents",
90:                 )
91:             ]
92:         }
93: 
94: 
95: ## Start of Pipeline
96: @observe(capture_input=False, capture_output=False)
97: @extract_fields(dict(mdl=Dict[str, Any]))
98: def validate_mdl(mdl_str: str, validator: MDLValidator) -> Dict[str, Any]:
99:     res = validator.run(mdl=mdl_str)
100:     return dict(mdl=res["mdl"])
101: 
102: 
103: @observe(capture_input=False)
104: def chunk(
105:     mdl: Dict[str, Any],
106:     chunker: ViewChunker,
107:     project_id: Optional[str] = None,
108: ) -> Dict[str, Any]:
109:     return chunker.run(mdl=mdl, project_id=project_id)
110: 
111: 
112: @observe(capture_input=False, capture_output=False)
113: async def embedding(chunk: Dict[str, Any], embedder: Any) -> Dict[str, Any]:
114:     return await embedder.run(documents=chunk["documents"])
115: 
116: 
117: @observe(capture_input=False, capture_output=False)
118: async def clean(
119:     embedding: Dict[str, Any],
120:     cleaner: DocumentCleaner,
121:     project_id: Optional[str] = None,
122: ) -> Dict[str, Any]:
123:     await cleaner.run(project_id=project_id)
124:     return embedding
125: 
126:

135: class HistoricalQuestion(BasicPipeline):
136:     def __init__(
137:         self,
138:         embedder_provider: EmbedderProvider,
139:         document_store_provider: DocumentStoreProvider,
140:         **kwargs,
141:     ) -> None:

143:         store = document_store_provider.get_store(dataset_name="view_questions")
144: 
145:         self._components = {
146:             "cleaner": DocumentCleaner([store]),
147:             "validator": MDLValidator(),
148:             "embedder": embedder_provider.get_document_embedder(),
149:             "chunker": ViewChunker(),
150:             "writer": AsyncDocumentWriter(
151:                 document_store=store,
152:                 policy=DuplicatePolicy.OVERWRITE,
153:             ),
154:         }
155:         self._configs = {}
156:         self._final = "write"
157: 
158:         super().__init__(
159:             AsyncDriver({}, sys.modules[__name__], result_builder=base.DictResult())
160:         )
161: 
162:     @observe(name="Historical Question Indexing")
163:     async def run(
164:         self, mdl_str: str, project_id: Optional[str] = None
165:     ) -> Dict[str, Any]:
166:         logger.info(
167:             f"Project ID: {project_id}, Historical Question Indexing pipeline is running..."
168:         )
169:         return await self._pipe.execute(
170:             [self._final],
171:             inputs={
172:                 "mdl_str": mdl_str,
173:                 "project_id": project_id,
174:                 **self._components,
175:                 **self._configs,
176:             },
177:         )
178:

## Assistant (Plan · GPT-5.3-Codex · 55.6s)

_Thinking:_



You understand core idea right.  
MDL + RAG yes. But full win comes from 3 context channels, not 1:
- schema context (models/relations/metrics/views)
- query memory (confirmed NL→SQL pairs)
- instructions/governance (business rules, allowed joins, metric definitions)

`Wren-style loop` = index once, retrieve per query, generate SQL, validate/dry-run, correct, execute, learn.

**Where sqlgent stands now**
- Strong base: graph pipeline + retry + SQL static safety in `src/agent/graph.py`, `src/agent/nodes.py`, `src/tools/database.py`.
- Missing semantic layer: no MDL parser/index/retriever.
- Missing learned memory: `previous_queries` only per request, no persisted NL→SQL recall.
- Missing semantic API: MCP has `ask_database`/`generate_sql`, no `prepare_semantics` flow in `src/mcp_server.py`.

**Best architecture for sqlgent (incremental, low risk)**
```text
MDL files -> compile to semantic docs -> embed/index
User question -> retrieve top-k schema + top-k NL->SQL examples + instructions
-> prompt LLM -> SQL -> static check + dry-run/execute
-> if fail: correction loop with error context
-> if success + confirmed: store NL->SQL pair
```

**Build plan**
1) Semantic model layer  
- Add minimal MDL schema first: models, columns, relationships, metrics, views, instructions.
- Keep Wren-compatible field names where possible. Migration pain lower later.

2) Indexing pipeline  
- New module `src/semantic/indexing.py`: MDL -> chunks (`TABLE`, `TABLE_COLUMNS`, `RELATION`, `METRIC`, `VIEW`, `INSTRUCTION`).
- Embed chunks, store with `project_id` + `type` metadata.
- Trigger manually first (`prepare_semantics` tool/command), not on every query.

3) Retrieval pipeline  
- New module `src/semantic/retrieval.py`.
- Hybrid retrieval: semantic vector + exact name boost.
- Token-budget packer: send only most useful chunks to prompt.
- Optional column pruning pass later (phase 2.5), not day 1.

4) Agent graph integration  
- Add node before `generate_query`: `retrieve_semantic_context`.
- Extend `AgentState` in `src/agent/state.py` with `semantic_context`, `few_shot_examples`, `project_id`.
- Update prompt templates in `src/agent/prompts.py` to include:
  - retrieved semantic DDL/context
  - retrieved similar NL→SQL examples
  - instruction block

5) Memory/self-learning  
- Persist confirmed pairs: `(project_id, question, sql, status, created_at, score?)`.
- Retrieve top similar pairs each query.
- Store only successful/approved queries. Bad pairs poison system.

6) API/MCP surface  
- Add tools in `src/mcp_server.py`:
  - `prepare_semantics(mdl, project_id)`
  - `ask_database(question, project_id, bypass_semantic_cache?)`
  - `confirm_sql_pair(question, sql, project_id)`
  - `invalidate_semantic_index(project_id|all)`

7) Evaluation + guardrails  
- Build small benchmark on Chinook/Northwind.
- Track: exec success rate, retry rate, empty/irrelevant rate, latency, token cost.
- Keep existing `SELECT/WITH only` guard. Add query timeout + row cap.

**MVP cuts (do first, skip fancy)**
- No intent classifier first.
- No chart generation first.
- No full Wren engine transpilation first.
- Focus on: MDL index + retrieval + SQL pair recall + correction loop.

**Why this path works**
- Reuses current graph and safety logic.
- Adds context where hallucination happens today.
- Ships useful version fast, then scales toward Wren depth.

Big design fork now: vector store backend.  
Recommend `Chroma local` first (fast ship, no infra), then adapter for `Qdrant` later.  
If you prefer, I draft exact file-by-file implementation plan next (new files, function signatures, state schema, node edges).

107: 
108: @observe(capture_input=False)
109: def filtered_documents(
110:     retrieval: dict,
111:     scope: str,
112:     scope_filter: ScopeFilter,
113:     score_filter: ScoreFilter,
114:     similarity_threshold: float,
115:     top_k: int,
116: ) -> dict:
117:     if not retrieval:
118:         return {}
119: 
120:     res = scope_filter.run(
121:         documents=retrieval.get("documents"),
122:         scope=scope,
123:     )
124: 
125:     return score_filter.run(
126:         documents=res.get("documents"),
127:         score=similarity_threshold,
128:         max_size=top_k,
129:     )
130: 
131: 
132: @observe(capture_input=False)
133: async def default_instructions(
134:     count_documents: int,
135:     retriever: Any,
136:     project_id: str,
137:     scope_filter: ScopeFilter,
138:     scope: str,
139: ) -> list[Document]:
140:     if not count_documents:
141:         return []
142: 
143:     filters = {
144:         "operator": "AND",
145:         "conditions": [
146:             {"field": "is_default", "operator": "==", "value": True},
147:         ],
148:     }
149: 
150:     if project_id:
151:         filters["conditions"].append(
152:             {"field": "project_id", "operator": "==", "value": project_id}
153:         )
154: 
155:     _res = await retriever.run(
156:         query_embedding=None,
157:         filters=filters,
158:     )
159: 
160:     res = scope_filter.run(
161:         documents=_res.get("documents"),
162:         scope=scope,
163:     )
164: 
165:     return dict(documents=res.get("documents"))
166: 
167: 
168: @observe(capture_input=False)
169: def formatted_output(
170:     default_instructions: list[Document],
171:     filtered_documents: dict,
172:     output_formatter: OutputFormatter,
173: ) -> dict:
174:     if not filtered_documents and not default_instructions:
175:         return {"documents": []}
176: 
177:     merged = default_instructions.get("documents") + filtered_documents.get("documents")
178:     documents = output_formatter.run(documents=merged)
179:     return documents
180: 
181: 
182: ## End of Pipeline
183: 
184: 
185: class Instructions(BasicPipeline):
186:     def __init__(
187:         self,
188:         embedder_provider: EmbedderProvider,
189:         document_store_provider: DocumentStoreProvider,
190:         similarity_threshold: float = 0.7,
191:         top_k: int = 10,
192:         **kwargs,
193:     ) -> None:
194:         store = document_store_provider.get_store(dataset_name="instructions")
195:         self._components = {
196:             "store": store,
197:             "embedder": embedder_provider.get_text_embedder(),
198:             "retriever": document_store_provider.get_retriever(
199:                 document_store=store,
200:             ),
201:             "scope_filter": ScopeFilter(),
202:             "score_filter": ScoreFilter(),
203:             "output_formatter": OutputFormatter(),
204:         }
205:         self._configs = {
206:             "similarity_threshold": similarity_threshold,
207:             "top_k": top_k,
208:         }
209: 
210:         super().__init__(
211:             AsyncDriver({}, sys.modules[__name__], result_builder=base.DictResult())
212:         )
213: 
214:     @observe(name="Instructions Retrieval")
215:     async def run(
216:         self, query: str, project_id: Optional[str] = None, scope: str = "sql"
217:     ):
218:         logger.info("Instructions Retrieval pipeline is running...")
219:         return await self._pipe.execute(
220:             ["formatted_output"],
221:             inputs={
222:                 "query": query,
223:                 "project_id": project_id or "",
224:                 "scope": scope,
225:                 **self._components,
226:                 **self._configs,
227:             },
228:         )

1: import asyncio
2: import logging
3: import sys
4: from typing import Any, Optional
5: 
6: from hamilton import base
7: from hamilton.async_driver import AsyncDriver
8: from haystack.components.builders.prompt_builder import PromptBuilder
9: from langfuse.decorators import observe
10: 
11: from src.core.pipeline import BasicPipeline
12: from src.core.provider import LLMProvider
13: from src.pipelines.common import clean_up_new_lines
14: from src.utils import trace_cost
15: from src.web.v1.services import Configuration
16: 
17: logger = logging.getLogger("wren-ai-service")
18: 
19: sql_to_answer_system_prompt = """
20: ### TASK
21: 
22: You are a data analyst that great at answering non-technical user's questions based on the data, sql so that even non technical users can easily understand.
23: Please answer the user's question in concise and clear manner in Markdown format.
24: 
25: ### INSTRUCTIONS
26: 
27: 1. Read the user's question and understand the user's intention.
28: 2. Read the sql and understand the data.
29: 3. Make sure the answer is aimed for non-technical users, so don't mention any technical terms such as SQL syntax.
30: 4. Generate a concise and clear answer in string format to answerthe user's question based on the data and sql.
31: 5. If answer is in list format, only list top few examples, and tell users there are more results omitted.
32: 6. Answer must be in the same language user specified.
33: 7. Do not include ```markdown or ``` in the answer.
34: 8. If the user provides a custom instruction, it should be followed strictly and you should use it to change the style of response.
35: 
36: ### OUTPUT FORMAT
37: 
38: Please provide your response in proper Markdown stringformat.
39: """
40: 
41: sql_to_answer_user_prompt_template = """
42: ### Inputs ###
43: User's question: {{ query }}
44: SQL: {{ sql }}
45: Data: 
46: columns: {{ sql_data.columns }}
47: rows: {{ sql_data.data }}
48: Language: {{ language }}
49: Current Time: {{ current_time }}
50: 
51: Custom Instruction: {{ custom_instruction }}
52: 
53: Please think step by step and answer the user's question.
54: """
55: 
56: 
57: ## Start of Pipeline
58: @observe(capture_input=False)
59: def prompt(
60:     query: str,
61:     sql: str,
62:     sql_data: dict,
63:     language: str,
64:     current_time: str,
65:     custom_instruction: str,
66:     prompt_builder: PromptBuilder,
67: ) -> dict:
68:     _prompt = prompt_builder.run(
69:         query=query,
70:         sql=sql,
71:         sql_data=sql_data,
72:         language=language,
73:         current_time=current_time,
74:         custom_instruction=custom_instruction,
75:     )
76:     return {"prompt": clean_up_new_lines(_prompt.get("prompt"))}
77: 
78: 
79: @observe(as_type="generation", capture_input=False)
80: @trace_cost
81: async def generate_answer(
82:     prompt: dict, generator: Any, query_id: str, generator_name: str
83: ) -> dict:
84:     return await generator(
85:         prompt=prompt.get("prompt"), query_id=query_id
86:     ), generator_name
87: 
88: 
89: ## End of Pipeline
90: 
91: 
92: class SQLAnswer(BasicPipeline):
93:     def __init__(
94:         self,
95:         llm_provider: LLMProvider,
96:         **kwargs,
97:     ):
98:         self._user_queues = {}
99:         self._components = {
100:             "prompt_builder": PromptBuilder(
101:                 template=sql_to_answer_user_prompt_template
102:             ),
103:             "generator": llm_provider.get_generator(
104:                 system_prompt=sql_to_answer_system_prompt,
105:                 streaming_callback=self._streaming_callback,
106:             ),
107:             "generator_name": llm_provider.get_model(),
108:         }
109: 
110:         super().__init__(
111:             AsyncDriver({}, sys.modules[__name__], result_builder=base.DictResult())
112:         )
113: 
114:     def _streaming_callback(self, chunk, query_id):
115:         if query_id not in self._user_queues:
116:             self._user_queues[
117:                 query_id
118:             ] = asyncio.Queue()  # Create a new queue for the user if it doesn't exist
119:         # Put the chunk content into the user's queue
120:         asyncio.create_task(self._user_queues[query_id].put(chunk.content))
121:         if chunk.meta.get("finish_reason"):
122:             asyncio.create_task(self._user_queues[query_id].put("<DONE>"))
123: 
124:     async def get_streaming_results(self, query_id):
125:         async def _get_streaming_results(query_id):
126:             return await self._user_queues[query_id].get()
127: 
128:         if query_id not in self._user_queues:
129:             self._user_queues[
130:                 query_id
131:             ] = asyncio.Queue()  # Ensure the user's queue exists
132:         while True:
133:             try:
134:                 # Wait for an item from the user's queue
135:                 self._streaming_results = await asyncio.wait_for(
136:                     _get_streaming_results(query_id), timeout=120
137:                 )
138:                 if (
139:                     self._streaming_results == "<DONE>"
140:                 ):  # Check for end-of-stream signal
141:                     del self._user_queues[query_id]
142:                     break
143:                 if self._streaming_results:  # Check if there are results to yield
144:                     yield self._streaming_results
145:                     self._streaming_results = ""  # Clear after yielding
146:             except TimeoutError:
147:                 break
148: 
149:     @observe(name="SQL Answer Generation")
150:     async def run(
151:         self,
152:         query: str,
153:         sql: str,
154:         sql_data: dict,
155:         language: str,
156:         current_time: str = Configuration().show_current_time(),
157:         query_id: Optional[str] = None,
158:         custom_instruction: Optional[str] = None,
159:     ) -> dict:
160:         logger.info("Sql_Answer Generation pipeline is running...")
161:         return await self._pipe.execute(
162:             ["generate_answer"],
163:             inputs={
164:                 "query": query,
165:                 "sql": sql,
166:                 "sql_data": sql_data,
167:                 "language": language,
168:                 "current_time": current_time,
169:                 "query_id": query_id,
170:                 "custom_instruction": custom_instruction or "",
171:                 **self._components,
172:             },
173:         )

1: import logging
2: from typing import Dict, List, Literal, Optional
3: 
4: from cachetools import TTLCache
5: from langfuse.decorators import observe
6: from pydantic import BaseModel
7: 
8: from src.core.pipeline import BasicPipeline
9: from src.pipelines.indexing.instructions import Instruction
10: from src.utils import trace_metadata
11: from src.web.v1.services import BaseRequest, MetadataTraceable
12: 
13: logger = logging.getLogger("wren-ai-service")
14: 
15: 
16: class InstructionsService:
17:     class Instruction(BaseModel):
18:         id: str
19:         instruction: str
20:         questions: List[str]
21:         # This is used to identify the default instruction needed to be retrieved for the project
22:         is_default: bool = False
23:         scope: Literal["sql", "answer", "chart"] = "sql"
24: 
25:     class Error(BaseModel):
26:         code: Literal["OTHERS"]
27:         message: str
28: 
29:     class Event(BaseModel, MetadataTraceable):
30:         event_id: str
31:         status: Literal["indexing", "deleting", "finished", "failed"] = "indexing"
32:         error: Optional["InstructionsService.Error"] = None
33:         trace_id: Optional[str] = None
34:         request_from: Literal["ui", "api"] = "ui"
35: 
36:     def __init__(
37:         self,
38:         pipelines: Dict[str, BasicPipeline],
39:         maxsize: int = 1_000_000,
40:         ttl: int = 120,
41:     ):
42:         self._pipelines = pipelines
43:         self._cache: Dict[str, self.Event] = TTLCache(maxsize=maxsize, ttl=ttl)
44: 
45:     # todo: move it to utils for super class?
46:     def _handle_exception(
47:         self,
48:         id: str,
49:         error_message: str,
50:         code: str = "OTHERS",
51:         trace_id: Optional[str] = None,
52:         request_from: Literal["ui", "api"] = "ui",
53:     ):
54:         self._cache[id] = self.Event(
55:             event_id=id,
56:             status="failed",
57:             error=self.Error(code=code, message=error_message),
58:             trace_id=trace_id,
59:             request_from=request_from,
60:         )
61:         logger.error(error_message)
62: 
63:     class IndexRequest(BaseRequest):
64:         event_id: str
65:         instructions: List["InstructionsService.Instruction"]
66: 
67:     @observe(name="Index Instructions")
68:     @trace_metadata
69:     async def index(
70:         self,
71:         request: IndexRequest,
72:         **kwargs,
73:     ):
74:         logger.info(
75:             f"Request {request.event_id}: Instructions Indexing process is running..."
76:         )
77:         trace_id = kwargs.get("trace_id")
78: 
79:         try:
80:             instructions = []
81:             for instruction in request.instructions:
82:                 if instruction.is_default:
83:                     instructions.append(
84:                         Instruction(
85:                             id=instruction.id,
86:                             instruction=instruction.instruction,
87:                             question="",
88:                             is_default=True,
89:                             scope=instruction.scope,
90:                         )
91:                     )
92:                 else:
93:                     for question in instruction.questions:
94:                         instructions.append(
95:                             Instruction(
96:                                 id=instruction.id,
97:                                 instruction=instruction.instruction,
98:                                 question=question,
99:                                 is_default=False,
100:                                 scope=instruction.scope,
101:                             )
102:                         )
103: 
104:             await self._pipelines["instructions_indexing"].run(
105:                 project_id=request.project_id,
106:                 instructions=instructions,
107:             )
108: 
109:             self._cache[request.event_id] = self.Event(
110:                 event_id=request.event_id,
111:                 status="finished",
112:                 trace_id=trace_id,
113:                 request_from=request.request_from,
114:             )
115: 
116:         except Exception as e:
117:             self._handle_exception(
118:                 request.event_id,
119:                 f"An error occurred during instructions indexing: {str(e)}",
120:                 trace_id=trace_id,

## Assistant (Plan · GPT-5.3-Codex · 56.4s)

_Thinking:_



Clear mode on for workflow sequence (avoid ambiguity). Caveman resume after.

**Wren Workflow (Start->Finish)**
- Split brain: Wren Engine = semantic planning/execution layer, Wren AI Service = RAG + prompting + correction orchestrator.
- Project starts with MDL (models, columns, relations, metrics, views). Engine/UI compiles/deploys manifest.
- Semantics prep runs once per MDL update: API triggers parallel indexing pipelines in `wren-ai-service/src/web/v1/services/semantics_preparation.py`.
- Indexing channels created: schema chunks (`db_schema.py`), table descriptions (`table_description.py`), historical questions (`historical_question.py`), SQL pairs (`sql_pairs.py`), project meta (`project_meta.py`).
- Each indexing pipeline does same pattern: validate -> chunk -> embed -> write vector store (Qdrant provider in `src/providers/document_store/qdrant.py`), scoped by `project_id`.
- Governance/instructions indexed through separate flow in `src/web/v1/services/instructions.py` + `src/pipelines/indexing/instructions.py`.
- User asks question: orchestrator in `src/web/v1/services/ask.py` moves statuses `understanding -> searching -> planning -> generating -> correcting -> finished/failed`.
- First retrieval chance: historical question recall; if very similar past view question exists, service can return SQL fast path.
- Parallel retrieval then runs for SQL few-shot pairs + instructions (`sql_pairs_retrieval.py`, `retrieval/instructions.py`).
- Core schema retrieval runs in `retrieval/db_schema_retrieval.py`: retrieve relevant table descriptions first, then fetch full schema docs for those tables.
- Context sizing rule: if schema small enough, send full relevant DDL; if big, run column-pruning selection step with LLM.
- SQL generation step (`generation/sql_generation.py`) builds prompt from: schema context + optional reasoning plan + SQL pairs + instructions + SQL functions + SQL knowledge.
- Engine dry-run/validation checks generated SQL before acceptance (post-process in generation pipeline, engine calls in `src/providers/engine/wren.py`).
- If invalid: correction loop runs (`sql_diagnosis` then `sql_correction.py`) up to configured retry budget.
- If still invalid: fail with `NO_RELEVANT_SQL`; if retrieval empty: fail with `NO_RELEVANT_DATA`.
- Separate execution/answer path exists: SQL executor runs query, SQL answer pipeline turns rows into user-friendly text (`retrieval/sql_executor.py`, `generation/sql_answer.py`).
- Feedback loop exists: user can send ask feedback to regenerate with constrained tables/reasoning (`services/ask_feedback.py`).
- Memory/self-learning loop: new SQL pairs/instructions can be indexed via services, then reused in later asks. This is how quality improves with usage.
- Net effect: offline indexing + online retrieval + guarded generation + correction + optional learning writeback.

**Tiny mental model**
- Index-time: build context memory.
- Query-time: retrieve smallest useful context.
- Generation-time: produce SQL with constraints.
- Validation-time: dry-run, correct, retry.
- Learning-time: store approved patterns.

Now caveman resume.

One lock question before we design sqlgent impl:  
For NL->SQL memory, what is success gate for storing pair?

1. Auto-store every executed SQL success.  
2. Store only when SQL executes + result non-empty + user confirms answer correct (recommended).  
3. Auto-store success, but mark `unverified`, promote to `verified` only after user confirmation.

Good call. Keep current flow. Add context as sidecar, not rewrite.

1. Add **verified NL->SQL memory** first.
- Store pair only when: SQL executed, result non-empty, user confirmed correct.
- Save fields: `project_id`, `db_fingerprint`, `question`, `sql`, `tables`, `created_at`, `status=verified`.
- Retrieve top-k similar verified pairs before `generate_query`. Inject as few-shot block.
- Keep toggle `ENABLE_QUERY_MEMORY` so old flow untouched.

2. Add **instructions/governance channel** second.
- Create instruction store: default rules + scoped rules (`sql`, later `answer`).
- Retrieve relevant rules per question, add to prompt.
- Keep hard safety in code (`SELECT/WITH` guard, timeout, row limit). Prompt not enough for safety.
- Add MCP ops: add/list/delete instructions.

3. Add **schema context channel** third.
- Start MDL-lite, not full Wren MDL.
- Minimal objects: models, columns, relationships, metrics, aliases/business terms.
- Index as chunks + embeddings. Retrieve top-k per query.
- Insert retrieval node before SQL generation. If no context hit, fallback to current table-name logic.

4. Add **evaluation harness** now, before heavy coding.
- Build fixed set (30-50 questions) on SQLite + MSSQL.
- Track: execution success, retry count, empty/irrelevant rate, latency, token cost.
- Run ablation: baseline vs +schema vs +memory vs +instructions. Big engineering value for PFE report.

5. Add **memory hygiene**.
- Dedup normalized `(question, sql)`.
- Forget endpoint for bad pairs.
- Optional decay/recency weighting so stale patterns fade.

6. Add **observability**.
- Log retrieved context IDs + similarity + selected few-shot pairs.
- Log correction-loop reason and final query provenance.
- Keep trace id from request to final answer.

Web UI question: build now or later?  
No, not now. Build backend context first. Then thin demo UI.  
Best UI after core ready: approve SQL pairs, manage instructions, inspect retrieved context per question.

One scope lock question:  
Schema context source, choose one:
1) Manual MDL-lite files only  
2) Auto-generate from live DB only  
3) Hybrid (recommended): auto-generate baseline + manual business overrides

## Assistant (Plan · GPT-5.3-Codex · 47.3s)

_Thinking:_



Plan-only. No code touched.

Good direction. Hybrid + backend-first + CLI feedback = strongest path.

**What add in sqlgent**
- `ADDED_CONTEXT_LAYER` big gate works. Recommend rename `ENABLE_CONTEXT_LAYER` (same meaning, cleaner).
- Keep sub-flags too: `ENABLE_SCHEMA_CONTEXT`, `ENABLE_QUERY_MEMORY`, `ENABLE_INSTRUCTIONS`, `ENABLE_FAILURE_LOG`.
- Keep current flow as fallback path when gate off. Zero regression risk.

**Should store failed requests?**
- Yes, but separate lane. Never feed failures as few-shot examples.
- Store per DB/project using `db_fingerprint` + `project_id`.
- Use for analytics + debugging only: top failing questions, wrong joins, bad columns, retry causes.
- Optional later: use failure patterns only in correction node as “avoid pattern”, not generation examples.

**Indexing phase (MDL + RAG)**
Current now:
- LLM picks table names.
- Agent pulls full schema + 1-row sample.
- Prompt -> SQL.

With hybrid MDL/RAG, indexing does this:
1. Read baseline schema from live DB introspection (tables, columns, PK/FK, types).  
2. If MDL files exist, load them (business names, definitions, join guidance, metrics, hidden fields, instructions).  
3. Merge baseline + MDL overrides into canonical semantic graph.  
4. Chunk into retrievable docs:
   - table summary docs
   - table columns docs
   - relationship/join docs
   - metric/view docs
   - instruction/governance docs
5. Embed chunks, upsert vector store with metadata:
   - `project_id`, `db_fingerprint`, `schema_hash`, `type`, `name`, `version`.
6. Re-index on schema/MDL change (manual command first, auto later).

**Query-time with 3 context channels**
- Question in.
- (Optional) existing table-name picker prefilters search space.
- Retrieve:
  - schema context chunks
  - verified NL->SQL pairs
  - instructions/governance docs
- Build token-budget context pack.
- Generate SQL -> validate/execute -> retry/correct if needed.
- CLI asks: “Result correct? [y/n]”
  - `y` + non-empty success -> store verified pair.
  - `n` -> do not store pair; if failure-log enabled, store failure event.

**Web UI now?**
- No. Backend first.
- Build thin UI later for:
  - approve/reject memory pairs
  - edit instructions
  - inspect retrieved context trace
- This order better engineering story for final-year project.

One clarify question (recommended default first):  
Use MDL as **YAML files in repo** (`semantic/`) with CLI scaffold + baseline auto-generated JSON cache (recommended), or use single JSON file only?  
Default changes: YAML => easier manual governance + review; JSON-only => simpler parser but weaker authoring flow.

## Assistant (Plan · GPT-5.3-Codex · 20.9s)

_Thinking:_



Good question. Big confusion point.

`DDL` = SQL language for **physical schema**.
- Commands like `CREATE TABLE`, `ALTER TABLE`, `CREATE VIEW`, `DROP INDEX`.
- Tells database how data stored/structured.
- In Wren-like RAG, DDL text often used as retrieval chunk because LLM reads SQL shape well.

Example DDL:
```sql
CREATE TABLE orders (
  order_id INT PRIMARY KEY,
  customer_id INT,
  amount DECIMAL(12,2),
  order_date DATE
);
```

`MDL` = **semantic/business model layer** above raw schema.
- Maps business concepts to physical tables/columns.
- Defines approved relationships/join paths.
- Defines reusable calculations/metrics.
- Adds aliases/descriptions/business terms.
- Can include governance hints (what to prefer/avoid).
- Not only “custom instruction text”.

Think like this:
- DDL = “how data stored”
- MDL = “what data means”
- Instructions = “how agent should behave”

For your hybrid plan:
- Baseline introspection -> auto semantic draft from DB schema.
- YAML MDL -> human/admin overrides business meaning.
- Merge both -> indexed context for retrieval.

So yes: MDL includes business logic, but also structure/relationships/metrics, not only instructions.

One lock question before implementation plan:
For `MDL v1`, include metrics/calculated fields now, or start simpler with `models + relationships + descriptions + instructions` then add metrics in phase 2?

18: def build_agent():

24: def display_streaming_content(content: str) -> None:

26: 
27: 
28: # FIX:fix prefilled text not working some environments (Different behavior of readline in Linux/Windows and terminal emulators )
29: async def get_user_input(interrupt_info) -> dict:
30:     print("\n" + "=" * 30)
31:     print("INTERRUPTED:")
32:     try:
33:         print(json.dumps(interrupt_info, indent=2))

35:         print(interrupt_info)
36: 
37:     prompt = "\nHow would you like to proceed? \n[a]ccept  [e]dit  [r]eject [f]eedback \nChoice: "
38:     choice = (await asyncio.to_thread(input, prompt)).strip().lower()
39: 
40:     if choice.startswith("e"):
41:         current_query = interrupt_info[0].get("args").get("query")
42: 
43:         def input_with_prefill(prompt, text):
44:             def hook():
45:                 readline.insert_text(text)
46:                 readline.redisplay()
47: 
48:             readline.set_pre_input_hook(hook)
49:             try:
50:                 return input(prompt)
51:             finally:
52:                 readline.set_pre_input_hook(None)
53: 
54:         new_query = await asyncio.to_thread(
55:             input_with_prefill, "Edit query: ", current_query
56:         )
57: 
58:         return {"type": "edit", "edited_query": new_query}
59: 
60:     elif choice.startswith("f"):
61:         feedback = await asyncio.to_thread(input, "Feedback for the agent: \n")
62:         return {"type": "response", "feedback": feedback}
63: 
64:     elif choice.startswith("r"):
65:         return {"type": "reject"}
66: 
67:     return {"type": "accept"}
68: 
69: 
70: async def run_agent(input_state: dict, config: dict):

72:     agent = build_agent()
73: 
74:     while True:
75:         resume_required = False
76:         async for mode, chunk in agent.astream(
77:             input_state,

81:             if mode == "messages":

95:                     if current_node:

97:         if not resume_required:
98:             break
99: 
100:     if AppConfig().SHOW_NODE_HISTORY:
101:         print("\n--- Full Node History ---")
102:         for i, state in enumerate(agent.get_state_history(config)):
103:             print(f"Checkpoint {i}: next={state.next} ")

105:

124:         "complex1": "Which 5 artists generated the most revenue, and what is their total revenue and number of tracks sold?",
125:         "complex2": "Which customers spent more than the average customer spending, and what is their total amount spent?",
126:         "northwind": "Find the top 3 employees who generated the highest total revenue from orders in 1997, including the employee’s full name, total revenue, and the number of distinct customers they served. Only include orders where the total order amount exceeds $5,000.",
127:     }
128:     question = questions["complex1"]

11:     GENERATE_QUERY,
12:     HANDLE_IRRELEVANT_RESULT,
13:     REGEN_PROMPTS,
14:     SHOULD_SKIP,
15: )
16: from agent.state import AgentState, AnalysisResult, SkipDecision
17: from config.app_config import AppConfig
18: from llm.model import model
19: from tools.database import (
20:     _get_config_flag,
21:     db,
22:     get_schema_tool_with_cache,
23:     list_tables_with_cache,
24:     run_query_tool_with_interrupt,
25: )
26: from utils.logger_setup import LoggerSetup
27: 
28: logger = LoggerSetup.get_logger(__name__, logging.INFO)
29: 
30: 
31: def list_tables(_state: AgentState, config: RunnableConfig | None = None):
32:     bypass_cache = _get_config_flag(
33:         config,
34:         "metadata_bypass_cache",
35:         AppConfig().METADATA_CACHE_BYPASS_DEFAULT,
36:     )
37:     invalidate_cache = _get_config_flag(config, "metadata_invalidate_cache", False)
38:     result = list_tables_with_cache(
39:         bypass_cache=bypass_cache,
40:         invalidate_cache=invalidate_cache,
41:     )
42: 
43:     logger.debug(f"List of tables in the database: {result}")
44:     if not result:
45:         raise ValueError("Failed to retrieve table names from the database.")
46: 
47:     response = AIMessage(f"Available tables: {', '.join(result)}")
48: 
49:     return {"messages": [response]}
50: 
51: 
52: get_schema_node = ToolNode([get_schema_tool_with_cache], name="get_schema")
53: 
54: 
55: def skip_pipeline(state: AgentState):
56:     system_message = {
57:         "role": "system",
58:         "content": SHOULD_SKIP,
59:     }
60:     structured_model = model.with_structured_output(SkipDecision)
61:     result = structured_model.invoke([system_message] + state["messages"])
62: 
63:     logger.debug(f"Skip Decision: {result}")
64:     return {
65:         "skip_decision": result,
66:     }
67: 
68: 
69: def should_skip(state: AgentState):
70:     if state["skip_decision"] and state["skip_decision"].skip:
71:         return "explain_result"
72: 
73:     return "call_get_schema"
74: 
75: 
76: def call_get_schema(state: AgentState):
77:     llm_with_tools = model.bind_tools([get_schema_tool_with_cache])
78: 
79:     if (
80:         state["analysis_result"] is not None
81:         and state["analysis_result"].status == "irrelevant"
82:     ):
83:         user_message = {
84:             "role": "user",
85:             "content": HANDLE_IRRELEVANT_RESULT.format(
86:                 user_question=state["user_question"],
87:                 query=state["last_query"],
88:                 database_output=state["messages"][-1].content,
89:                 explanation=state["analysis_result"].explanation,
90:             ),
91:         }
92:         response = llm_with_tools.invoke([user_message] + state["messages"])
93:         current_retry_count = state["retry_count"] + 1
94:         return {"messages": [response], "retry_count": current_retry_count}
95: 
96:     response = llm_with_tools.invoke(state["messages"])
97:     logger.debug(f"Schema Tool Response: {response}")
98:     return {"messages": [response]}
99: 
100: 
101: def generate_query(state: AgentState):
102:     system_message = {
103:         "role": "system",
104:         "content": GENERATE_QUERY.format(
105:             dialect=db.dialect,
106:             top_k=5,
107:         ),
108:     }
109:     llm_with_tools = model.bind_tools(
110:         [run_query_tool_with_interrupt], tool_choice="any"
111:     )
112: 
113:     if state["analysis_result"] is not None and state["analysis_result"].status in [
114:         "error",
115:         "empty_result",
116:     ]:
117:         original_messages = [m for m in state["messages"] if m.type == "human"]
118:         user_message = {
119:             "role": "user",
120:             "content": REGEN_PROMPTS[state["analysis_result"].status].format(
121:                 query=state["last_query"],
122:                 explanation=state["analysis_result"].explanation,
123:             ),
124:         }
125: 
126:         response = llm_with_tools.invoke(
127:             [system_message] + original_messages + [user_message]
128:         )
129: 
130:         current_retry_count = state["retry_count"] + 1
131:         return {"messages": [response], "retry_count": current_retry_count}
132: 
133:     response = llm_with_tools.invoke([system_message] + state["messages"])
134:     logger.debug(f"Generated Query: {response}")
135: 
136:     return {"messages": [response]}
137: 
138: 
139: def check_query(state: AgentState):
140:     system_message = {
141:         "role": "system",
142:         "content": CHECK_QUERY.format(dialect=db.dialect),
143:     }
144: 
145:     tool_call = state["messages"][-1].tool_calls[0]
146:     user_message = {"role": "user", "content": tool_call["args"]["query"]}
147:     llm_with_tools = model.bind_tools(
148:         [run_query_tool_with_interrupt], tool_choice="any"
149:     )
150:     response = llm_with_tools.invoke([system_message, user_message])
151:     response.id = state["messages"][-1].id
152:     last_query = response.tool_calls[0]["args"]["query"]
153: 
154:     logger.debug(f"Check Query Response: {response}")
155:     logger.debug(f"Checked Query: {last_query}")
156: 
157:     return {
158:         "messages": [response],
159:         "last_query": last_query,
160:         "previous_queries": [last_query],
161:     }
162: 
163: 
164: def should_execute(_state: AgentState):
165:     if not AppConfig().EXECUTE_SQL_QUERIES:
166:         return "explain_result"
167:     return "run_query"
168: 
169: 
170: run_query_node = ToolNode([run_query_tool_with_interrupt], name="run_query")
171: 
172: 
173: def analyze_result(state: AgentState):
174:     db_output = state["messages"][-1].content or "Empty, 0 rows returned"
175: 
176:     system_message = {
177:         "role": "system",
178:         "content": ANALYZE_RESULT.format(
179:             user_input=state["user_question"],
180:             query_executed=state["last_query"],
181:             database_output=db_output,
182:             retry_count=state["retry_count"],
183:         ),
184:     }
185:     structured_llm = model.with_structured_output(AnalysisResult)
186: 
187:     response = structured_llm.invoke([system_message])
188: 
189:     logger.debug(f"Analysis Result: {response.model_dump_json()}")
190: 
191:     return {
192:         "db_output": db_output,
193:         "analysis_result": response,
194:     }
195: 
196: 
197: def should_retry(state: AgentState):
198:     analysis_result = state.get("analysis_result")
199: 
200:     if state["retry_count"] < AppConfig().MAX_SQL_RETRIES:
201:         if analysis_result.status == "irrelevant":
202:             return "call_get_schema"
203:         if analysis_result.status in ["error", "empty_result"]:
204:             return "generate_query"
205:         # explicit exit to prevent repeated queries
206:         if (
207:             len(state["previous_queries"]) > 1
208:             and state["last_query"] in state["previous_queries"][:-1]
209:         ):
210:             return "explain_result"
211: 
212:     return "explain_result"
213: 
214: 
215: def explain_result(state: AgentState):
216:     if not AppConfig().EXECUTE_SQL_QUERIES:
217:         explanation = f"SQL execution is disabled. Skipping query . Here is last generated query: {state['last_query']}"
218:         logger.debug(f"No Execution:{explanation}")
219:         return {"messages": [AIMessage(content=explanation)]}
220: 
221:     if state["skip_decision"] and state["skip_decision"].skip:
222:         explanation = f"The agent decided to skip executing the query because: {state['skip_decision'].reason}"
223:         logger.debug(f"Skip Explanation: {explanation}")
224:         return {"messages": [AIMessage(content=explanation)]}
225: 
226:     system_message = {
227:         "role": "system",
228:         "content": EXPLAIN_RESULT.format(
229:             user_question=state["user_question"],
230:             query=state["last_query"],
231:             status=state["analysis_result"].status,
232:             database_output=state["db_output"],
233:             explanation=state["analysis_result"].explanation,
234:         ),
235:     }
236:     response = model.invoke([system_message] + state["messages"])
237:     logger.debug(f"Explanation: {response}")
238:     return {"messages": [response]}

2: You are an agent designed to interact with a SQL database.
3: Given an input question, create a syntactically correct {dialect} query to run,

7:

17: 
18: """

20: CHECK_QUERY = """

26: - Data type mismatch in predicates
27: - Properly quoting identifiers

49: 3. IRRELEVANT: Data returned does not match the semantic intent of the question.
50: 4. EMPTY_RESULT: Query executed successfully but returned 0 rows.
51: 
52: INSTRUCTIONS:

55: - If IRRELEVANT: Explain which columns/data were wrong.

60: OUTPUT FORMAT:

83: Follow these instructions based on the status:

85: - success: Present the results clearly and concisely in natural language. Summarize key findings, highlight important values, and format tables or lists if needed.

87: - empty_result: Inform the user that the query ran successfully but returned no matching data. Suggest that the data they are looking for may not exist in the database, or that their question may need to be rephrased.

89: - irrelevant: Show the returned data to the user and explain that while the query executed, the results may not fully match their intent. Ask the user if this is close to what they were looking for or if they would like to refine the question.

91: - error: Apologize and inform the user that the query could not be executed due to an internal error after multiple attempts. Avoid technical jargon. Suggest they rephrase their question or contact support.

1: import logging
2: 
3: from langchain.tools import tool
4: from langchain_community.tools.sql_database.tool import (
5:     InfoSQLDatabaseTool,
6:     QuerySQLDatabaseTool,
7: )
8: from langchain_community.utilities import SQLDatabase
9: from langchain_core.runnables import RunnableConfig
10: from langgraph.types import interrupt
11: from sqlglot import exp, parse
12: 
13: from config.app_config import AppConfig
14: from config.db_config import DBConfig
15: from tools.metadata_cache import (
16:     get_cached_metadata,
17:     invalidate_metadata_cache,
18:     set_cached_metadata,
19: )
20: from utils.logger_setup import LoggerSetup
21: 
22: logger = LoggerSetup.get_logger(__name__, logging.INFO)
23: 
24: 
25: ALLOWED = (
26:     exp.Select,
27:     exp.With,
28: )
29: 
30: # SQLAlchemy and sqlglot use differant  names for some dialects
31: SQLGLOT_COMPLIANET_DIALECTS = {
32:     "mssql": "tsql",
33:     "postgresql": "postgres",
34:     "mariadb": "mysql",
35: }
36: 
37: 
38: def validate_sql(query: str, dialect: str = None) -> bool:
39:     try:
40:         statements = parse(query, read=dialect)
41: 
42:     except Exception as e:
43:         logger.error(f"Parser Error: {e}")
44:         return False
45: 
46:     if len(statements) != 1:
47:         return False
48: 
49:     ast = statements[0]
50: 
51:     if not isinstance(ast, ALLOWED):
52:         return False
53: 
54:     return True
55: 
56: 
57: def _build_database_components():
58:     database_uri = DBConfig().get_database_uri()
59:     database = SQLDatabase.from_uri(database_uri, sample_rows_in_table_info=1)
60:     get_schema = InfoSQLDatabaseTool(db=database)
61:     run_query = QuerySQLDatabaseTool(db=database)
62: 
63:     return database, get_schema, run_query
64: 
65: 
66: db, get_schema_tool, run_query_tool = _build_database_components()
67: 
68: 
69: def _get_config_flag(
70:     config: RunnableConfig | None, flag: str, default: bool = False
71: ) -> bool:
72:     if not config:
73:         return default
74:     configurable = config.get("configurable") if isinstance(config, dict) else None
75:     if not isinstance(configurable, dict):
76:         return default
77:     return bool(configurable.get(flag, default))
78: 
79: 
80: def list_tables_with_cache(
81:     *, bypass_cache: bool = False, invalidate_cache: bool = False
82: ) -> list[str]:
83:     if invalidate_cache:
84:         invalidate_metadata_cache(DBConfig().get_database_uri())
85: 
86:     cached = get_cached_metadata(
87:         operation="list_tables",
88:         operation_args=None,
89:         bypass_cache=bypass_cache,
90:     )
91:     if cached is not None:
92:         return list(cached)
93: 
94:     table_names = db.get_usable_table_names()
95:     set_cached_metadata(
96:         operation="list_tables",
97:         operation_args=None,
98:         value=table_names,
99:         bypass_cache=bypass_cache,
100:     )
101:     return table_names
102: 
103: 
104: @tool(
105:     get_schema_tool.name,
106:     description=get_schema_tool.description,
107:     args_schema=get_schema_tool.args_schema,
108: )
109: def get_schema_tool_with_cache(config: RunnableConfig, **tool_input):
110:     bypass_cache = _get_config_flag(config, "metadata_bypass_cache", False)
111:     operation_args = {"table_names": tool_input.get("table_names", "")}
112: 
113:     cached = get_cached_metadata(
114:         operation="get_schema",
115:         operation_args=operation_args,
116:         bypass_cache=bypass_cache,
117:     )
118:     if cached is not None:
119:         return cached
120: 
121:     tool_response = get_schema_tool.invoke(tool_input, config)
122:     set_cached_metadata(
123:         operation="get_schema",
124:         operation_args=operation_args,
125:         value=tool_response,
126:         bypass_cache=bypass_cache,
127:     )
128:     return tool_response
129: 
130: 
131: @tool(
132:     run_query_tool.name,
133:     description=run_query_tool.description,
134:     args_schema=run_query_tool.args_schema,
135: )
136: def run_query_tool_with_interrupt(config: RunnableConfig, **tool_input):
137:     # static check
138:     dialect = SQLGLOT_COMPLIANET_DIALECTS.get(db.dialect, db.dialect)
139:     if not validate_sql(tool_input["query"], dialect=dialect):
140:         raise ValueError(
141:             "Failed the static check. Only SELECT and WITH statements are allowed. No multiple statements allowed."
142:         )
143:     # human interruption
144:     if not AppConfig().HUMAN_SQL_REVIEW:
145:         final_query_input = tool_input
146:     else:
147:         request = {
148:             "action": run_query_tool.name,
149:             "args": tool_input,
150:             "description": "Please review the tool call",
151:         }
152:         response = interrupt([request])
153:         if response["type"] == "accept":
154:             final_query_input = tool_input
155:         elif response["type"] == "edit":
156:             tool_input = response["edited_query"]
157:             final_query_input = {"query": tool_input}
158:         elif response["type"] == "response":
159:             return f"User cancelled the query and provided this feedback: {response['feedback']}"
160: 
161:         elif response["type"] == "reject":
162:             raise RuntimeError("User rejected the tool call")
163:         else:
164:             raise ValueError(f"Unsupported interrupt response type: {response['type']}")
165:     try:
166:         tool_response = run_query_tool.invoke(final_query_input, config)
167:         return tool_response
168: 
169:     except Exception as e:
170:         return (
171:             f"Error executing SQL: {str(e)}\n"
172:             "Please analyze this error, correct the query, and try again."
173:         )
